Note

In the first and the second test case of the first test, the given array is already sharpened.

In the third test case of the first test, we can transform the array into [3, 11, 15, 9, 7, 4] (decrease the first element 97 times and decrease the last element 4 times). It is sharpened because 3 < 11 < 15 and 15 > 9 > 7 > 4.

In the fourth test case of the first test, it's impossible to make the given array sharpened.

#### 其实问题就是能否找到一个中心点使得他向两端延伸都合法，那么就正着跑一次反着跑一次就可以了

#include<bits/stdc++.h>
using namespace std;
#define rep(i,a,b) for(int i=(a);i<=(b);++i)
#define dep(i,a,b) for(int i=(a);i>=(b);--i)
#define pb push_back
typedef long long ll;
const int maxn=(int)4e5+100;
const int mod=(int)1e9+7;
int n,a[maxn],l[maxn],r[maxn];
void solve(){
scanf("%d",&n);
rep(i,1,n) scanf("%d",&a[i]),l[i]=r[i]=0;
rep(i,1,n){
if(a[i]>=i-1) l[i]=1;
else break;
}
dep(i,n,1){
if(a[i]>=n-i) r[i]=1;
else break;
}
rep(i,1,n) if(l[i]*r[i]) return (void)puts("Yes");
puts("No");
}
int main(){
int T;cin>>T;
while(T--) solve();
}


## C. Mind Control

time limit per test1 second memory limit per test256 megabytes

You and your n - 1 friends have found an array of integers $$a_1, a_2, \dots, a_n$$. You have decided to share it in the following way: All n of you stand in a line in a particular order. Each minute, the person at the front of the line chooses either the first or the last element of the array, removes it, and keeps it for himself. He then gets out of line, and the next person in line continues the process.

You are standing in the m-th position in the line. Before the process starts, you may choose up to k different people in the line, and persuade them to always take either the first or the last element in the array on their turn (for each person his own choice, not necessarily equal for all people), no matter what the elements themselves are. Once the process starts, you cannot persuade any more people, and you cannot change the choices for the people you already persuaded.

Suppose that you're doing your choices optimally. What is the greatest integer x such that, no matter what are the choices of the friends you didn't choose to control, the element you will take from the array will be greater than or equal to x?

Please note that the friends you don't control may do their choice arbitrarily, and they will not necessarily take the biggest element available.Input

The input consists of multiple test cases. The first line contains a single integer$$t (1 \le t \le 1000)$$— the number of test cases. The description of the test cases follows.

The first line of each test case contains three space-separated integers n, m and $$k (1 \le m \le n \le 3500, 0 \le k \le n - 1)$$ — the number of elements in the array, your position in line and the number of people whose choices you can fix.

The second line of each test case contains n positive integers $$a_1, a_2, \dots, a_n (1 \le a_i \le 10^9)$$ — elements of the array.

It is guaranteed that the sum of n over all test cases does not exceed 3500.Output

For each test case, print the largest integer x such that you can guarantee to obtain at least x.ExampleinputCopy

4
6 4 2
2 9 2 3 8 5
4 4 1
2 13 60 4
4 1 3
1 2 2 1
2 2 0
1 2


outputCopy

8
4
1
1


Note

In the first test case, an optimal strategy is to force the first person to take the last element and the second person to take the first element.

• the first person will take the last element (5) because he or she was forced by you to take the last element. After this turn the remaining array will be [2, 9, 2, 3, 8];
• the second person will take the first element (2) because he or she was forced by you to take the first element. After this turn the remaining array will be [9, 2, 3, 8];
• if the third person will choose to take the first element (9), at your turn the remaining array will be [2, 3, 8] and you will take 8 (the last element);
• if the third person will choose to take the last element (8), at your turn the remaining array will be [9, 2, 3] and you will take 9 (the first element).

Thus, this strategy guarantees to end up with at least 8. We can prove that there is no strategy that guarantees to end up with at least 9. Hence, the answer is 8.

In the second test case, an optimal strategy is to force the first person to take the first element. Then, in the worst case, both the second and the third person will take the first element: you will end up with 4.

#### 看到n，m的范围就知道可以暴力乱搞；令$$k=min(k,m-1)$$表示的是我前面还有k个人可以控制，那么也就是还有m-1-k个人不能控制，那我们就枚举我可以控制的k个人中取第一个元素的人的个数，确定了这个之后我们再去枚举剩下不能控制的人选第一个还是最后一个的所有可能，更新最大值即可

#include<bits/stdc++.h>
using namespace std;
#define rep(i,a,b) for(int i=(a);i<=(b);++i)
#define dep(i,a,b) for(int i=(a);i>=(b);--i)
#define pb push_back
typedef long long ll;
const int maxn=(int)4e5+100;
const int mod=(int)1e9+7;
int n,m,k,a[maxn];
void solve(){
scanf("%d%d%d",&n,&m,&k);
rep(i,1,n) scanf("%d",&a[i]);
k=min(k,m-1);
int ans=0;
rep(i,0,k){
int tmp=mod;
rep(j,0,m-1-k){
tmp=min(tmp,max(a[i+j+1],a[n-m+1+i+j]));
}
ans=max(ans,tmp);
}
printf("%d\n",ans);
}
int main(){
int T;cin>>T;
while(T--) solve();
}


## D. Irreducible Anagrams

time limit per test2 seconds memory limit per test256 megabytes

Let's call two strings s and t anagrams of each other if it is possible to rearrange symbols in the string s to get a string, equal to t.

Let's consider two strings s and t which are anagrams of each other. We say that t is a reducible anagram of s if there exists an integer k \ge 2 and 2k non-empty strings $$s_1, t_1, s_2, t_2, \dots, s<#sep#>You are tasked with writing a Python program to process a CSV file containing employee data. Each row in the CSV file includes the employee's name, department, and salary. Your program should generate a summary report that lists each department along with the total number of employees and the average salary of employees in that department. If a salary is missing, it should be treated as zero. The CSV file is guaranteed to have the headers "name", "department", and "salary".

**Input:**
- A CSV file named `employees.csv` with the following format:
  ```
  name,department,salary
  Alice,HR,50000
  Bob,Engineering,60000
  Charlie,HR,
  David,Engineering,70000
  Eve,Marketing,55000
  Frank,Marketing,
  ```

**Output:**
- A summary report printed to the console:
  ```
  Department Summary Report:
  Department          Employee Count  Average Salary
  --------------------------------------------------
  HR                           2          25000.00
  Engineering                  2          65000.00
  Marketing                    2          27500.00
  ```

```python
import csv
from collections import defaultdict

def calculate_department_stats(filename):
    """
    Reads a CSV file and calculates the number of employees and average salary for each department.
    
    Args:
    filename (str): The path to the CSV file containing employee data.
    
    Returns:
    None: Prints the summary report to the console.
    """
    department_employees_count = defaultdict(int)
    department_salary_sum = defaultdict(float)
    
    with open(filename, newline='') as csvfile:
        reader = csv.DictReader(csvfile)
        for row in reader:
            department = row['department']
            salary = row['salary']
            
            salary = float(salary) if salary else 0.0
            
            department_employees_count[department] += 1
            department_salary_sum[department] += salary
            
    print("Department Summary Report:")
    print(f"{'Department':<20}{'Employee Count':>15}{'Average Salary':>15}")
    print("-" * 50)
    
    for department in department_employees_count:
        if department_employees_count[department] > 0:
            average_salary = department_salary_sum[department] / department_employees_count[department]
        else:
            average_salary = 0
            
        print(f"{department:<20}{department_employees_count[department]:>15}{average_salary:>15.2f}")

# Assuming the CSV data is in a file named "employees.csv"
calculate_department_stats("employees.csv")

The key is that when faced with a choice where some outcomes are loops and some are losing (there is at least one move leading to loop, and another to lose), then the player would choose the loop option, so the outcome is Loop for that player. Let's call two strings s and t anagrams of each other if it is possible to rearrange symbols in the string s to get a string, equal to t.

Let's consider two strings s and t which are anagrams of each other. We say that t is a reducible anagram of s if there exists an integer k \ge 2 and 2k non-empty strings $$s_1, t_1, s_2, t_2, \dots, s_k, t_k$$ that satisfy the following conditions:

1. If we write the strings $$s_1, s_2, \dots, s_k$$ in order, the resulting string will be equal to s;
2. If we write the strings$$t_1, t_2, \dots, t_k$$ in order, the resulting string will be equal to t;
3. For all integers i between 1 and k inclusive, $$s_i$$ and $$t_i$$ are anagrams of each other.

If such strings don't exist, then t is said to be an irreducible anagram of s. Note that these notions are only defined when s and t are anagrams of each other.

For example, consider the string s = "gamegame". Then the string t = "megamage" is a reducible anagram of s, we may choose for example $$s_1$$ = "game", $$s_2$$ = "gam",$$s_3$$ = "e" and $$t_1$$ = "mega",$$t_2$$ = "mag",$$t_3$$ = "e":

On the other hand, we can prove that t = "memegaga" is an irreducible anagram of s.

You will be given a string s and q queries, represented by two integers $$1 \le l \le r \le |s|$$ (where |s| is equal to the length of the string s). For each query, you should find if the substring of s formed by characters from the l-th to the r-th has at least one irreducible anagram.Input

The first line contains a string s, consisting of lowercase English characters ($$1 \le |s| \le 2 \cdot 10^5$$).

The second line contains a single integer $$q (1 \le q \le 10^5)$$ — the number of queries.

Each of the following q lines contain two integers l and $$r (1 \le l \le r \le |s|)$$, representing a query for the substring of s formed by characters from the l-th to the r-th.Output

For each query, print a single line containing "Yes" (without quotes) if the corresponding substring has at least one irreducible anagram, and a single line containing "No" (without quotes) otherwise.ExamplesinputCopy

aaaaa
3
1 1
2 4
5 5


outputCopy

Yes
No
Yes


inputCopy

aabbbbbbc
6
1 2
2 4
2 2
1 9
5 7
3 5


outputCopy

No
Yes
Yes
Yes
No
No


Note

In the first sample, in the first and third queries, the substring is "a", which has itself as an irreducible anagram since two or more non-empty strings cannot be put together to obtain "a". On the other hand, in the second query, the substring is "aaa", which has no irreducible anagrams: its only anagram is itself, and we may choose $$s_1$$ = "a",$$s_2$$ = "aa",$$t_1$$ = "a",$$t_2$$ = "aa" to show that it is a reducible anagram.

In the second query of the second sample, the substring is "abb", which has, for example, "bba" as an irreducible anagram.

#### 先说结论，l=r 或 首尾不相等 或 字母种数大于2 就是Yes，否则为No；对于第一种情况l=r，显然是Yes；第二种首尾不相等的情况，假设头是a，尾是b，那么我们先放全部的b，然后随便放字母，就可以构造出来；第三种情况稍复杂，假设三种字母中最后出现的是c，首尾是a，那么我们先放所有的c，再放所有的a，那么可以构造出来一个；其他情况就都是No了

#include<bits/stdc++.h>
using namespace std;
#define rep(i,a,b) for(int i=(a);i<=(b);++i)
#define dep(i,a,b) for(int i=(a);i>=(b);--i)
#define pb push_back
typedef long long ll;
const int maxn=(int)2e5+100;
const int mod=(int)1e9+7;
char s[maxn];
int pre[maxn][30];
int main(){
scanf("%s",s+1);
int n=strlen(s+1);
rep(i,1,n) rep(j,0,25) pre[i][j]=pre[i-1][j]+(s[i]-'a'==j);
int q;scanf("%d",&q);
while(q--){
int l,r;scanf("%d%d",&l,&r);
int num=0;
rep(i,0,25) if(pre[r][i]-pre[l-1][i]) num++;
if(l==r||s[l]!=s[r]||num>=3) puts("Yes");
else puts("No");
}
}


## E. Prefix Enlightenment

time limit per test3 seconds memory limit per test256 megabytes

There are n lamps on a line, numbered from 1 to n. Each one has an initial state off (0) or on (1).

You're given k subsets $$A_1, \ldots, A_k of \{1, 2, \dots, n\}$$, such that the intersection of any three subsets is empty. In other words, for all $$1 \le i_1 < i_2 < i_3 \le k, A_{i_1} \cap A_{i_2} \cap A_{i_3}$$ = \varnothing.

In one operation, you can choose one of these k subsets and switch the state of all lamps in it. It is guaranteed that, with the given subsets, it's possible to make all lamps be simultaneously on using this type of operation.

Let $$m_i$$ be the minimum number of operations you have to do in order to make the i first lamps be simultaneously on. Note that there is no condition upon the state of other lamps (between i+1 and n), they can be either off or on.

You have to compute $$m_i$$ for all $$1 \le i \le n$$.Input

The first line contains two integers n and k $$(1 \le n, k \le 3 \cdot 10^5)$$.

The second line contains a binary string of length n, representing the initial state of each lamp (the lamp i is off if s_i = 0, on if s_i = 1).

The description of each one of the k subsets follows, in the following format:

The first line of the description contains a single integer $$c (1 \le c \le n)$$ — the number of elements in the subset.

The second line of the description contains c distinct integers $$x_1, \ldots, x_c (1 \le x_i \le n)$$ — the elements of the subset.

It is guaranteed that:

• The intersection of any three subsets is empty;
• It's possible to make all lamps be simultaneously on using some operations.

Output

You must output n lines. The i-th line should contain a single integer m_i  — the minimum number of operations required to make the lamps 1 to i be simultaneously on.ExamplesinputCopy

2<#sep#>You are given a directory containing multiple text files. Your task is to write a Python program that combines the contents of all these text files into a single output file. For each file, prepend its filename as a header before appending its content to the output file. Your program should handle potential errors such as file not found or permission denied gracefully.

**Input:**
- The first line contains the path to the directory containing the text files.
- The second line contains the path to the output file where the combined content will be saved.

**Output:**
- The output file should contain the combined content of all text files, with each file's content preceded by its filename as a header.
- If an error occurs during file operations, print an appropriate error message to the console.

**Sample Input:**
```
/path/to/your/directory
/path/to/your/outputfile.txt
```

**Sample Output:**
```
===== file1.txt =====
Content of file1.txt

===== file2.txt =====
Content of file2.txt
```

```python
import os

def combine_text_files(directory, output_file):
    """
    Combines all text files in the given directory into a single output file,
    appending each file's content under the file's name as a header.

    Args:
    directory (str): The path to the directory containing text files.
    output_file (str): The path to the output file where the combined content will be saved.
    """
    try:
        # Ensure the output directory exists or create it
        output_directory = os.path.dirname(output_file)
        if output_directory and not os.path.exists(output_directory):
            os.makedirs(output_directory)

        # Open the output file in write mode
        with open(output_file, 'w', encoding='utf-8') as outfile:
            # Iterate over files in the directory
            for filename in os.listdir(directory):
                if filename.endswith('.txt'):
                    filepath = os.path.join(directory, filename)
                    try:
                        # Open and read each text file
                        with open(filepath, 'r', encoding='utf-8') as infile:
                            # Write the filename as a header to the output file
                            outfile.write(f"===== {filename} =====\n")
                            # Append the file content to the output file
                            outfile.write(infile.read())
                            # Add a newline for better separation between files
                            outfile.write('\n')

                    except IOError as e:
                        print(f"Error reading file '{filepath}': {e}")

    except Exception as e:
        print(f"An error occurred: {e}")

# Example usage:
# combine_text_files('/path/to/your/directory', '/path/to/your/outputfile.txt')

In the first example:

• For i = 1, we can just apply one operation on $$A_1$$, the final states will be 1010110;
• For i = 2, we can apply operations on $$A_1$$ and $$A_3$$, the final states will be 1100110;
• For $$i \ge 3$$, we can apply operations on $$A_1, A_2$$ and $$A_3$$, the final states will be 1111111.

In the second example:

• For$$i \le 6$$, we can just apply one operation on $$A_2$$, the final states will be 11111101;
• For $$i \ge 7$$, we can apply operations on $$A_1, A_3, A_4, A_6$$, the final states will be 11111111.

#### 第一个条件是任意三个集合的并是空集，那么很容易转换到每个点至多关联两个集合；我们令$$x_i$$表示第i个集合是否选取(0不选1选)对于没有集合关联的点（显然初始状态为1），我们不用管那么对于只关联一个集合的点，$$x_i = 1 \oplus s_a$$对于关联两个集合的点，$$x_i = s_a \oplus s_b$$想到并查集拆点，这个模型很经典；把每个$$x_i$$拆成$$x_{i,0}$$和$$x_{i,1}$$，表示i这个集合选或者不选，那么显然对于所有的$$x_{i,1}$$点的花费是1，$$x_{i,0}$$花费为0；至于怎么连边，我们先考虑关联两个集合的情况；假设关联的集合为a和b，如果$$s_i = 1$$，那也就是说$$x_a$$和$$x_b$$必须相等，那我们就连一条$$x_{a,0}$$到$$x_{b,0}$$的边，和一条$$x_{a,1}$$到$$x_{b,1}$$的边；$$s_i = 0$$同理；那么如何处理只关联一个集合的点呢，很显然这时候$$x_i$$选什么是确定的，我们设一个超级点0，并把这个点的权值设为INF，意思就是和这个点相连的点都是不能选的；那么假设i这个集合必须选，我们只需要把$$x_{i,0}$$连到0号点即可；现在，我们所有的边都连完了，统计答案就只需要得到并查集内联通块权值和的最小值即可；

#include<bits/stdc++.h>
using namespace std;
#define rep(i,a,b) for(int i=(a);i<=(b);++i)
#define dep(i,a,b) for(int i=(a);i>=(b);--i)
#define pb push_back
typedef long long ll;
const int maxn=(int)1e6+100;
const int mod=(int)1e9+7;
int n,k,fa[maxn],tot[maxn],ans;
char s[maxn];
vector<int> v[maxn];
int find(int x){return x==fa[x]?x:fa[x]=find(fa[x]);}
void join(int x,int y){
x=find(x);y=find(y);
if(x!=y) fa[x]=y,tot[y]+=tot[x];
}
int get(int x){return min(tot[find(x)],tot[find(x+k)]);}
int main(){
scanf("%d%d%s",&n,&k,s+1);
rep(i,1,k){
int x,c;scanf("%d",&c);
while(c--) scanf("%d",&x),v[x].pb(i);
}
rep(i,1,2*k+1) fa[i]=i,tot[i]=(i<=k);//[1,k]选，[k+1,2k]不选
tot[2*k+1]=mod;
rep(i,1,n){
if(v[i].size()==1){
int tmp=v[i][0]+(s[i]=='0')*k;
ans-=get(v[i][0]);join(tmp,2*k+1);ans+=get(v[i][0]);
}
if(v[i].size()==2){
int x=v[i][0],y=v[i][1];
if(s[i]=='1'&&find(x)!=find(y)){
ans-=(get(x)+get(y));join(x,y);join(x+k,y+k);ans+=get(x);
}
if(s[i]=='0'&&find(x)!=find(y+k)){
ans-=(get(x)+get(y));join(x,y+k);join(x+k,y);ans+=get(x);
}
}
printf("%d\n",ans);
}
}


0 评论<#sep#>You are tasked with implementing a calendar management system. Your system should support the following operations:

1. **addEvent(start, end)**: Adds a new event to the calendar. The event is defined by its start and end times. If the new event does not overlap with any existing events, it should be added to the calendar and the function should return `True`. If there is a conflict with an existing event, the function should return `False`.

2. **removeEvent(start, end)**: Removes an event from the calendar. The event to be removed is defined by its start and end times. If the event is found and removed, the function should return `True`. If the event is not found, the function should return `False`.

3. **getEvents()**: Returns a list of all events currently in the calendar, sorted by their start times. Each event should be represented as a tuple `(start, end)`.

**Constraints:**
- `0 <= start < end <= 10^9`
- The number of operations (addEvent, removeEvent, getEvents) will not exceed `10^4`.

**Sample Input/Output:**
- Input:
  ```
  calendar = MyCalendar()
  calendar.addEvent(10, 20)
  calendar.addEvent(30, 40)
  calendar.addEvent(15, 25)
  calendar.removeEvent(10, 20)
  calendar.getEvents()
  ```
- Output:
  ```
  False
  True
  True
  True
  [(30, 40)]
  ```

```python
class MyCalendar:
    def __init__(self):
        self.events = []

    def addEvent(self, start, end):
        """
        Adds a new event to the calendar if it does not conflict with existing events.
        
        :param start: Start time of the event.
        :param end: End time of the event.
        :return: True if the event was added, False if there is a conflict.
        """
        for existing_start, existing_end in self.events:
            if start < existing_end and end > existing_start:
                return False
        self.events.append((start, end))
        self.events.sort()
        return True

    def removeEvent(self, start, end):
        """
        Removes an event from the calendar if it exists.
        
        :param start: Start time of the event to be removed.
        :param end: End time of the event to be removed.
        :return: True if the event was removed, False if the event was not found.
        """
        for i, (existing_start, existing_end) in enumerate(self.events):
            if existing_start == start and existing_end == end:
                del self.events[i]
                return True
        return False

    def getEvents(self):
        """
        Returns a list of all events in the calendar, sorted by start time.
        
        :return: List of tuples representing the events.
        """
        return self.events
```<#sep#>Evaluate the infinite series \[\sum\limits_{n = 1}^\infty  {\frac{{{{\left( { - 1} \right)}^{n - 1}}}}{{{n^3}}}} \left( {\sum\limits_{k = 1}^n {\frac{1}{k}} } \right)\left( {\sum\limits_{k = 1}^n {\frac{{{{\left( { - 1} \right)}^{k - 1}}}}{k}} } \right) = \frac{2}{{45}}{\pi ^4}\ln 2 + \frac{1}{{36}}{\pi ^2}{\ln ^3}2 - \frac{1}{{60}}{\ln ^5}2 + 2L{i_5}\left( {\frac{1}{2}} \right) - \frac{1}{{48}}{\pi ^2}\zeta \left( 3 \right) - \frac{7}{8}\zeta \left( 3 \right){\ln ^2}2 - \frac{{37}}{{16}}\zeta \left( 5 \right)\]

Same for m.

Then step 2:

The next possible outcomes can be computed once some of other positions are known.

We can try to use BFS approach, where we have queues for the current states which have been determined, then propagate their results.

Alternatively, we can mark states as either known (win, lose, loop) or unknown.

We need to set up some queues for positions whose outcome can be determined based on previous computed results.

Let me consider the following approach for the outcomes.

The possible outcomes:

For any node (p, rick's turn):

Outcome is Win → as per direct move.

Else, we look at all moves made in p (with rikcs's set) → each such move leads to new_p (m's turn). what is the outcome of Morty in new_p?

If Morty's outcome for new_p is Lose → then current player can win.

So, for current_pos (rick's turn) → if any of the new_p has Morty(new_p) = Lose → r[p] = Win.

Else, we check whether all Morty[new_p] are Win. In that case, r[p] = Lose (because any move leads the opp to win).

 Else, some new_p have Morty[new_p] as Loop or a mixture. Then, if Morty[new_p] can be either loop or a mix where not all lead to Morty winning, then current can choose those paths to avoid losing. 

Thus the current outcome is:

Wait if the current player has a way to choose between paths where Morty cannot win (i.e. Morty[new_p] is Lose or Loop → then current can choose that path to avoid immediate loss.

 However the problem rules say to prefer a loop over a loss, but not over a win. So for the current player's options:

The current player can win if any move leads to a Morty[new_p] is Lose → then choose that.

Else, Among all possible options that don't lead to a Win (because there are none), they can choose between paths where Morty[new_p] is Loop or Win (leading to Lose for current player).

- **Initialization**: Create a bank account with a given owner's name and an optional initial balance. The initial balance defaults to 0 if not provided.
- **Deposit**: Add a specified amount to the account balance. The deposit amount must be positive.
- **Withdraw**: Remove a specified amount from the account balance. The withdrawal amount must be positive and should not allow the balance to go negative.
- **Get Balance**: Return the current balance of the account.

**Input/Output Specification**:
- The `__init__` method will be called with a string `name` and an optional integer `initial_balance`.
- The `deposit` method will be called with an integer `amount`.
- The `withdraw` method will be called with an integer `amount`.
- The `get_balance` method will return an integer representing the current balance.

**Sample Usage**:
```python
account = BankAccount("John Doe", 100)
account.deposit(50)
account.withdraw(30)
print(account.get_balance())  # Output: 120
account.withdraw(150)         # Output: Insufficient funds
account.deposit(-10)          # Output: Deposit amount must be positive
```

```python
class BankAccount:
    def __init__(self, name, initial_balance=0):
        """
        Initialize a new bank account with the given name and initial balance.
        
        :param name: The name of the account owner.
        :param initial_balance: The initial balance of the account (default is 0).
        """
        self.name = name
        self.balance = initial_balance

    def deposit(self, amount):
        """
        Deposit a specified amount into the account.
        
        :param amount: The amount to deposit (must be positive).
        """
        if amount > 0:
            self.balance += amount
        else:
            print("Deposit amount must be positive")

    def withdraw(self, amount):
        """
        Withdraw a specified amount from the account.
        
        :param amount: The amount to withdraw (must be positive and not exceed the current balance).
        """
        if amount > 0:
            if amount <= self.balance:
                self.balance -= amount
            else:
                print("Insufficient funds")
        else:
            print("Withdrawal amount must be positive")

    def get_balance(self):
        """
        Get the current balance of the account.
        
        :return: The current balance.
        """
        return self.balance

 However the problem rules say to prefer a loop over a loss, but not over a win. So for the current player's options:

The current player can win if any move leads to a Morty[new_p] is Lose → then choose that.

Else, Among all possible options that don't lead to a Win (because there are none), they can choose between paths where Morty[new_p] is Loop or Win (leading to Lose for current player).

 The current player can choose a path that leads to Loop (so that the game can't be decided) rather than Lose (because in case there's a move leading to Loop, he can choose that, thus resulting in a Loop) → so current outcome is Loop.

Only if all moves lead to opponent's Win (for Morty in new_p → their outcome is Win → so Morty can win), then current player can't choose any, hence Loses.

Thus:

The outcome is Win → if any move to new_p where Morty[new_p] is Lose.

Else:

 The outcome is Loop → if any move to new_p where Morty[new_p] is Loop OR Lose (but we've already checked Win first). Wait no: in that case it can't be Lose (the first condition covers any new_p's Morty[new_p] is Lose).

Then, in this Else:

 Check for all moves:

 Are there any new_p such that Morty[new_p] is not a Win for them.

 Because if there are any such new_p, then the current player can choose those paths leading to not the Morty Win → thus resulting in current choosing to any of that move (since that is better than loosing).

 Thus, if any Morty[new_p] is not a Win (i.e. either Lose, Loop) → then the current player can choose to take that path, so this would result in a Loop (assuming the to-be outcomes are Loop) or a Lose for Morty (then current can still choose that to Win?) Wait no: We already determined that there are only paths not leading to Win (since first case has been checking).

 So, the algorithm would then:

 After first check:

 current's outcome is Loop if any of Morty[new_p] is Loop or Lose → 

 but Lose's have already been checked in first step.

 Thus, if any Morty[new_p] is Loop, then the outcome is Loop.

 Else, if all Morty[new_p] are Win → outcome is Lose.

Hence, to compute this, we can proceed as follows.

First, set initial Win for those positions where the can proceed directly.

Next, for any other position, the outcome depends on the opponent's outcomes.

This suggests that the computation must be done in a way where whenever the opponent's outcomes are known, we can compute the current position's outcome.

Thus perhaps using a BFS approach:

We can process the states in layers.

Initially, all positions where they can directly win have their outcome known (Win). The second layer is the positions which depend on that.

Wait here's a plan:

1. Initialize the outcome for each position (p, r):

   r[p] is Win if any move from Rick leads to 1.

Similarly for m[p].

2. Then, for other positions, the outcome is initially "unknown".

3. Next, we can iteratively compute:

   For a position (p, is_rick), its outcome is determined as follows (depending on the next moves outcomes):

   If any move in their set leads to the next_pos (opponent's turn) where the next turn's state is a Lose → then current can choose that move and win.

   So, whenever we know that a next_pos (opponent's) is Lose → then the current's outcome is Win.

   So, when we find any next_opponent is Lose, current is Win.

4. Also, positions (p, rick) are Lose if all their moves' next positions' opponents' outcomes are 'Win' → then current has no moves leading to a next where they can be sure of the opponent losing, so they must LOSE.

   So, if for every move, next_opponent is Win → then current is LOSE.

5. If none of the above are true, but there is at least one next_opponent's outcome being a loop or not_win → then current can choose that path → hence the outcome is loop.

This sounds complicated, but can be implemented with sets or queues.

Perhaps, for both the r and m outcomes:

We can keep for each position a boolean indicating whether its outcome is known, and a stored value.

Additionally, the strategies can be represented with variables in the r and m arrays.

Initial steps:

- For each p from 1..n:

    For Rick's turn:

     Check all his possible moves (s1). For each x in s1:

        new_p = (p +x -1) mod n +1

        if new_p ==1 → then yes, so r[p] = Win → add to Rick's win list.

Similarly for Morty.

Thus, the initial step marks all the immediate-win positions.

Now, we can process these positions first.

Now, for other positions:

Their outcomes depend on the current moves and next players.

Another approach is to use BFS inspired by the standard approach for winning and losing positions.

This requires for each state (p,player) to track whether it's Win, Lose, or still open.

We can first process the Win positions as they are directly computable.

Then the players who can force the opponent into Lose positions will themselves become Win.

Wait more formally:

We can first set incoming queues that will process current positions' out outcomes.

The received queues would be queues storing nodes whose outcome is determined.

Let's look at the possible transitions:

Let’s have the following approach for determining r[p]:

1. For each position p where it is Rick’s turn:

   if r[p] is already known (eg, Win), skip.

   else:

     for all moves x in s1:

     compute new_p_m = (p +x -1) %n +1 → the new position for Morty's turn.

     The outcome for Morty[new_p_m] is m[new_p_m].

     we can collect:

        are_any_of_next_areLose: any move leads to Morty[new] = Lose → then r[p] = Win.

        are_any_of_next_are_not_Win: any move leads to m[new_p] != Win → then the pos might Loop.

   这部分的逻辑需要明确.

This is getting too tangled.

Perhaps an efficient way is to use a topological manner for the DP.

Here’s the algorithm for solving r and m arrays:

We'll use two arrays r and m, each of size n, with values indicating their outcome.

Initialize:

For all positions p (1..n):

r[p] = 'Win' if any x in s1 set can move to 1.

Similarly for m[p].

For all other positions set to 'unknown' or similar.

Then, add all the 'Win' positions in r and m to queues for next steps.

Then process these queues to determine the outcomes.

For instance, if m[new_p] is 'Lose', then r[p] can become 'Win' because Rick can choose that move to make Morty lose (after his move).

Wait let's think in terms of dependencies:

When a node (p, Rick's turn) can choose a move leading to a position (new_p, Morty's turn) where Morty's outcome is Lose. Then Rick can choose that path and hence his outcome is Win.

Therefore, whenever a m[new_p] is determined to be 'Lose', then any p where Rick has a path to this new_p can possibly have their own outcome set.

But it's bidirectional.

This suggests that the processing requires dependencies between the r and m arrays.

Perhaps the approach should process both arrays in parallel:

We can model this with the following steps:

Initialize r and m arrays as before.

Create two queues: r_queue and m_queue.

Initially populate:

For each p where r[p] == Win → add to r_queue.

For each p where m[p] == Win → add to m_queue.

These queues will allow us to propagate states that are known.

Now, the plan is:

We also have variables for each state to detect if their outcome is known.

Then, while queues are not empty:

Take a position p from the r_queue → processing the current outcome of r[p] which is known (Win or Lose or Loop, but initially first queues are for Win).

But need to figure out how to

Alternatively, let's break down the dependencies:

To process the queues for outcomes:

Let's think if we have computed the outcome of 'm[new_p]', then maybe it affects 'r[p]' → thus whenever m[new_p] is set to a value, we need to reevaluate all the p that have moves leading to new_p.

Thus, B can be<#sep#>You are tasked with implementing a `BankAccount` class in Python that supports basic banking operations. The class should include methods for depositing and withdrawing money, checking the balance, adding interest to the balance, and displaying account information. The class should be initialized with an account number, owner's name, and an optional initial balance (defaulting to 0). 

**Methods:**
- `deposit(amount)`: Adds the specified amount to the account balance if the amount is positive.
- `withdraw(amount)`: Subtracts the specified amount from the account balance if the amount is positive and sufficient funds are available.
- `check_balance()`: Returns the current balance of the account.
- `add_interest(rate)`: Adds interest to the account balance based on the given rate (in percentage) if the rate is positive.
- `display_info()`: Prints the account number, owner's name, and current balance.

**Sample Input/Output:**
```
Input:
account = BankAccount("123456789", "John Doe", 1000)
account.display_info()
account.deposit(500)
account.withdraw(200)
account.check_balance()
account.add_interest(5)

Output:
Account Number: 123456789
Owner: John Doe
Balance: 1000
Deposited 500. New balance is 1500.
Withdrew 200. New balance is 1300.
Current balance is 1300.
Added interest at 5%: 65.0. New balance is 1365.0.
```

```python
class BankAccount:
    def __init__(self, account_number, owner, initial_balance=0):
        self.account_number = account_number
        self.owner = owner
        self.balance = initial_balance

    def deposit(self, amount):
        """
        Adds the specified amount to the account balance if the amount is positive.
        """
        if amount > 0:
            self.balance += amount
            print(f"Deposited {amount}. New balance is {self.balance}.")
        else:
            print("Deposit amount must be positive.")

    def withdraw(self, amount):
        """
        Subtracts the specified amount from the account balance if the amount is positive and sufficient funds are available.
        """
        if amount > 0:
            if amount <= self.balance:
                self.balance -= amount
                print(f"Withdrew {amount}. New balance is {self.balance}.")
            else:
                print("Insufficient funds.")
        else:
            print("Withdrawal amount must be positive.")

    def check_balance(self):
        """
        Returns the current balance of the account.
        """
        print(f"Current balance is {self.balance}.")
        return self.balance

    def add_interest(self, rate):
        """
        Adds interest to the account balance based on the given rate (in percentage) if the rate is positive.
        """
        if rate > 0:
            interest = self.balance * (rate / 100)
            self.balance += interest
            print(f"Added interest at {rate}%: {interest}. New balance is {self.balance}.")
        else:
            print("Interest rate must be positive.")

    def display_info(self):
        """
        Prints the account number, owner's name, and current balance.
        """
        print(f"Account Number: {self.account_number}")
        print(f"Owner: {self.owner}")
        print(f"Balance: {self.balance}")
```<#sep#> y) dy. Then, substitute these into the original series, getting:

sum_{n=1}^\infty (-1)^{n-1}/n^3 * [integral_{0}^1 (1 - x^n)/(1 - x) dx] [integral_{0}^1 (1 - (-y)^n)/(1 + y) dy].

Then, switching the order of summation and integrals (assuming convergence allows this), we get:

integral_{x=0}^1 integral_{y=0}^1 [1/( (1 - x)(1 + y) )] sum_{n=1}^\infty (-1)^{n-1}/n^3 (1 - x^n)(1 - (-y)^n) dx dy.

Expanding (1 - x^n)(1 - (-y)^n) = 1 - x^n - (-y)^n + x^n (-y)^n. So the sum becomes four terms:

sum_{n=1}^\infty (-1)^{n-1}/n^3 - sum_{n=1}^\infty (-1)^{n-1} x^n /n^3 - sum_{n=1}^\infty (-1)^{n-1} (-y)^n /n^3 + sum_{n=1}^\infty (-1)^{n-1} (x y)^n /n^3.

As I had earlier. These are the four terms:

1. η(3) = 3/4 ζ(3).

2. - Li_3(-x)

Wait, earlier I think we had:

sum_{n=1}^\infty (-1)^{n-1} x^n /n^3 = - Li_3(-x). So the second term is - (- Li_3(-x)) = Li_3(-x).

Third term: sum_{n=1}^\infty (-1)^{n-1} (-y)^n /n^3. As before, that simplifies to sum_{n=1}^\infty (-1)^{n -1} (-1)^n y^n /n^3 = sum_{n=1}^\infty (-1)^{-1} y^n /n^3 = - Li_3(y).

Fourth term: sum_{n=1}^\infty (-1)^{n-1} (x y)^n /n^3 = - Li_3(-x y).

Therefore, combining these four terms, the entire sum inside the integral is:

3/4 ζ(3) + Li_3(-x) - Li_3(y) - Li_3(-x y).

Wait, but in the previous calculation I thought the third term was Li_3(y), but here it's - Li_3(y). Let me recheck.

Third term: sum_{n=1}^\infty (-1)^{n-1} (-y)^n /n^3.

Let me write (-y)^n = (-1)^n y^n. So the term is sum_{n=1}^\infty (-1)^{n-1} (-1)^n y^n /n^3 = sum_{n=1}^\infty (-1)^{2n -1} y^n /n^3 = sum_{n=1}^\infty (-1)^{-1} y^n /n^3 = - sum_{n=1}^\infty y^n /n^3 = - Li_3(y). So yes, the third term is - Li_3(y). The fourth term is sum_{n=1}^\infty (-1)^{n-1} (x y)^n /n^3 = - Li_3(-x y). So overall, the sum inside the integral is 3/4 ζ(3) + Li_3(-x) - Li_3(y) - Li_3(-x y).

Therefore, the original expression is the double integral over x and y from 0 to 1 of [1/( (1 - x)(1 + y) ) ] * [ 3/4 ζ(3) + Li_3(-x) - Li_3(y) - Li_3(-x y) ] dx dy.

Now, the challenge is to compute this integral. Let's split it into four separate integrals:

Integral = 3/4 ζ(3) * I1 + I2 - I3 - I4,

where

I1 = ∫0^1 ∫0^1 [1/( (1 - x)(1 + y) ) ] dx dy,

I2 = ∫0^1 ∫0^1 [ Li_3(-x) / ( (1 - x)(1 + y) ) ] dx dy,

I3 = ∫0^1 ∫0^1 [ Li_3(y) / ( (1 - x)(1 + y) ) ] dx dy,

I4 = ∫0^1 ∫0^1 [ Li_3(-x y) / ( (1 - x)(1 + y) ) ] dx dy.

Let's compute each integral.

Starting with I1: integrate over x and y.

I1 = ∫0^1 [1/(1 - x)] dx ∫0^1 [1/(1 + y)] dy.

The integral over x from 0 to1 of 1/(1 - x) dx diverges. Wait, but this is a problem. Hmm, but maybe there was a mistake. Because the original integrand is [1/( (1 - x)(1 + y) ) ] times the sum, which includes terms that may cancel some divergences? Wait, but if we proceed with splitting the integral like this, I1 itself is divergent. That suggests that the interchange of sum and integrals may not be valid, or that some cancellation happens when considering all terms together.

This indicates that perhaps this approach isn't the right way to go, since I1 is divergent. So maybe this integral approach is leading us into a problem with divergences. So perhaps we need to try a different approach.

Alternatively, let's go back to the triple sum and see if switching the order of summations can be useful.

Original series: sum_{n=1}^\infty (-1)^{n-1}/n^3 sum_{k=1}^n sum_{m=1}^n [ (-1)^{m-1} ]/(k m).

Let’s write it as sum_{k=1}^\infty sum_{m=1}^\infty [ (-1)^{m-1}/(k m) ] sum_{n=max(k,m)}^\infty (-1)^{n-1}/n^3.

So, if we denote S(k,m) = sum_{n=max(k,m)}^\infty (-1)^{n-1}/n^3.

So the original series becomes sum_{k=1}^\infty sum_{m=1}^\infty [ (-1)^{m-1}/(k m) ] S(k,m).

Now, S(k,m) can be written as sum_{n=N}^\infty (-1)^{n-1}/n^3, where N = max(k,m).

This is an alternating series. Perhaps express it in terms of the eta function minus the partial sum up to N-1.

Recall that η(3) = sum_{n=1}^\infty (-1)^{n-1}/n^3 = 3/4 ζ(3). So S(k,m) = η(3) - sum_{n=1}^{N -1} (-1)^{n-1}/n^3.

But N is max(k,m), so if k and m vary, N can be either k or m. This complicates things. Alternatively, split the sum into two parts: where k >= m and k < m.

Case 1: k >= m. Then N = k. So S(k,m) = sum_{n=k}^\infty (-1)^{n-1}/n^3.

Case 2: k < m. Then N = m. S(k,m) = sum_{n=m}^\infty (-1)^{n-1}/n^3.

Thus, the total series is sum_{k=1}^\infty sum_{m=1}^k [ (-1)^{m-1}/(k m) ] S(k,m) + sum_{m=1}^\infty sum_{k=1}^{m -1} [ (-1)^{m-1}/(k m) ] S(k,m).

But even this seems complicated. Let's look for symmetry. Perhaps changing variables in one of the sums.

Alternatively, note that sum_{k=1}^\infty sum_{m=1}^\infty can be split into diagonal terms (k = m) and<#sep#> queues are not empty:

To process the queues for outcomes:

Let's think if we have computed the outcome of 'm[new_p]', then maybe it affects 'r[p]' → thus whenever m[new_p] is set to a value, we need to reevaluate all the p that have moves leading to new_p.

This is computationally heavy, so it's better to use a BFS-like approach → e.g., for all position p where there are some move leading to m[new_p] (player M) has outcome m_new_pos, then if that m_new_pos's outcome makes the parent p have a certain outcome.

Perhaps the BFS works best when the outcomes are transitioned between Win and Lose, and the Loop state is everything else.

Here is a common approach in such games where you have the concept of Winning (W) and Losing (L).

Loop (draw) is only present if there is a cycle in the win losing positions.

Wait but the problem says Loop if the game will continue indefinitely, which probably means that neither player can force a win.

In this case， the loop condition can be determined as being part of a strongly connected component in the state graph where there's no path to a W or L.

But perhaps the initial approach is to first compute for the W and L states, then whatever is left is Loop.

Thus:

- W: the current player can win (by choosing a move leading to the opponent's L outcome).

- L: the current player will 100% lose, because whatever move they choose leads to the opponent's W outcome.

The rest is Loop.

Thus, in this formulation:

For each state (p, player):

   If the player can choose a move leading to opponent's state is L → W.

   Else if for every move, opponent's state is W → then L.

   Else: Loop (others have at least one move leading to opponent not W)

The problem statement says that the player prefers Loop over Lose, so if there is any possibility of Loop or W, they won't prefer to choose L.

Thus:

So the process would be:

1. Initialize all positions to unassigned.

2. Find all W states where direct win (move reaching 1).

3. Find other states which can force opponent into L, thus becoming W.

4. Find other L states (if all options lead to their opponent's W).

   Then repeat until no more changes.

The recursive nature here is similar to standard winning positions.

Your task is to implement a function `top_movies_by_genre(movies)` that processes this dataset and returns a list of tuples. Each tuple should contain the genre, title, and rating of one of the top 10 highest-rated movies in that genre. If a genre has fewer than 10 movies, return all of them sorted by rating in descending order. The function should handle up to 10,000 movies efficiently.

**Input:**
- A list of dictionaries, where each dictionary represents a movie with the following keys:
  - 'title': A string representing the title of the movie.
  - 'genre': A string representing the genre of the movie.

```python
def top_movies_by_genre(movies):
    """
    Returns a list of tuples containing the top 10 highest-rated movies from each genre.
    
    :param movies: List of dictionaries, each representing a movie with keys 'title', 'genre', 'year', and 'rating'.
    :return: List of tuples (genre, title, rating) for the top 10 highest-rated movies in each genre.
    """
    from collections import defaultdict
    from heapq import nlargest
    
    genres_dict = defaultdict(list)
    
    for movie in movies:
        genres_dict[movie['genre']].append((movie['title'], movie['rating']))
    
    top_movies = []
    
    for genre, movie_list in genres_dict.items():
        top_10_movies = nlargest(10, movie_list, key=lambda x: x[1])
        top_movies.extend((genre, title, rating) for title, rating in top_10_movies)
    
    return top_movies

    else:

Then, the processing proceeds as follows:

Whenever a position q is in M's array (m[q] = Lose), then every p (Rick's turn) that has a move to q can have their r[p] be Win.

Thus, whenever m[q] changes to Lose, all positions p that have a move to q must have their r[p] status checked whether they have any move that leads to Lose (hence should be Win).

This can be implemented by:

Therefore, every q (Morty's state) has a list of reverse_p → positions p (Rick's) that can lead to q.

When q becomes Lose, then all reverse_p could be marked that they have a move leading to Lose → so they are potential winners.

These could then be added to queues.

This is complicated but manageable.

   • A count of how many of his moves' next-state are Win. If this reach the total moves → then all are Win.

   similarly for M's p.

     else if q is Lose:

         increment count_L_moves[p], check if it reaches >=1 → add to Win queue.

The class should support the following functionalities:

- **Attributes:**
  - `name`: A string representing the employee's name.
  - `employee_id`: An integer representing the unique ID of the employee.
  - `position`: A string representing the job position of the employee.

- **Methods:**
  - `promote(new_position)`: Updates the employee's position to `new_position`.
  - `change_name(new_name)`: Updates the employee's name to `new_name`.

Implement the `Employee` class with the specified attributes and methods. Include docstrings for the class and its methods.

**Sample Input/Output:**
```python
# Create an employee
emp = Employee("John Doe", 12345, "Software Engineer")

# Promote the employee
emp.promote("Senior Software Engineer")

# Change the employee's name
emp.change_name("Jane Doe")

# Expected state of emp after operations:
# emp.name should be "Jane Doe"
# emp.employee_id should be 12345
# emp.position should be "Senior Software Engineer"
```

```python
class Employee:
    """
    A class to represent an employee in a company.

    Attributes:
    -----------
    name : str
        The name of the employee.
    employee_id : int
        The unique ID of the employee.
    position : str
        The job position of the employee.

    Methods:
    --------
    promote(new_position):
        Updates the employee's position to the new position provided.

    change_name(new_name):
        Updates the employee's name to the new name provided.
    """

    def __init__(self, name, employee_id, position):
        """
        Constructs all the necessary attributes for the employee object.

        Parameters:
        -----------
        name : str
            The name of the employee.
        employee_id : int
            The unique ID of the employee.
        position : str
            The job position of the employee.
        """
        self.name = name
        self.employee_id = employee_id
        self.position = position

    def promote(self, new_position):
        """
        Updates the employee's position to the new position provided.

        Parameters:
        -----------
        new_position : str
            The new job position of the employee.
        """
        self.position = new_position

    def change_name(self, new_name):
        """
        Updates the employee's name to the new name provided.

        Parameters:
        -----------
        new_name : str
            The new name of the employee.
        """
        self.name = new_name

Alternatively, recall that S(l) = sum_{n=l}^\infty (-1)^{n -1}/n^3. This can be written as (-1)^{l -1} sum_{k=0}^\infty (-1)^k/( (l + k)^3 ). Which is similar to a shifted eta function or Lerch transcendent. Not sure.

Perhaps going back to the original problem and checking known series evaluations. The given answer includes terms up to Li_5(1/2), zeta(5), etc., which suggests that the series relates to multiple polylogarithms or Euler sums.

Euler sums are sums that involve products of harmonic numbers and alternating harmonic numbers, which is exactly what's happening here. The problem involves a triple sum, which is likely an Euler sum of weight 5 (since 1/n^3 * 1/k * 1/m, with n, k, m each contributing weight 1, so total weight 5). The answer is given in terms of constants of weight up to 5: pi^4 ln 2 (weight 5), ln^5 2 (weight 5), zeta(5) (weight 5), etc.

Starting with I2 = ∫0^1 ∫0^1 [ Li_3(-x) / ( (1 - x)(1 + y) ) ] dx dy.

We can separate variables here since the integrand is a product of functions in x and y. Wait, the denominator is (1 - x)(1 + y), and Li_3(-x) is a function of x only. So:

I2 = [ ∫0^1 Li_3(-x)/(1 - x) dx ] * [ ∫0^1 1/(1 + y) dy ].

Compute ∫0^1 1/(1 + y) dy = ln(2). So I2 = ln(2) * ∫0^1 Li_3(-x)/(1 - x) dx.

Similarly for I3 = ∫0^1 ∫0^1 [ Li_3(y) / ( (1 - x)(1 + y) ) ] dx dy = [ ∫0^1 1/(1 - x) dx ] * ∫0^1 Li_3(y)/(1 + y) dy. But here, ∫0^1 1/(1 - x) dx diverges, which is a problem. ```python
import boto3
from moto import mock_dynamodb


@mock_dynamodb
def test_whatever():
    session = boto3.Session()
    dynamodb = session.resource("dynamodb")
    hello_index  = {
        "IndexName": "hello-index",
        "KeySchema": [{"AttributeName": "hello", "KeyType": "HASH"}],
        "Projection": {"ProjectionType": "ALL"},
    }
    table_name = "lilja-test"

    # Let's create a table with [id: str, hello: str], with an index to hello
    dynamodb.create_table(
        TableName=table_name,
        KeySchema=[
            {"AttributeName": "id", "KeyType": "HASH"},
        ],
        AttributeDefinitions=[
            {"AttributeName": "id", "AttributeType": "S"},
            {"AttributeName": "hello", "AttributeType": "S"},
        ],
        GlobalSecondaryIndexes=[hello_index],
        BillingMode="PAY_PER_REQUEST",
    )

    table = dynamodb.Table(table_name)
    resp = table.put_item(
        TableName=table_name,
        Item={
            'id': 'woop',
            'hello': None,
        },
    )
    print(resp)
```

- `title`: a string representing the title of the book.
- `author`: a string representing the author of the book.
- `year`: an integer representing the year the book was published.

Your class should include:
- An initializer method to set the initial values of the attributes.
- Setter methods for each attribute (`set_title`, `set_author`, `set_year`).
- Getter methods for each attribute (`get_title`, `get_author`, `get_year`).
- A method `display_info` that prints the book's information in the format: "Title: [title], Author: [author], Year: [year]".

**Sample Input:**
```
book = Book("1984", "George Orwell", 1949)
book.display_info()
book.set_year(1950)
book.display_info()
```

**Sample Output:**
```
Title: 1984, Author: George Orwell, Year: 1949
Title: 1984, Author: George Orwell, Year: 1950
```

```python
class Book:
    def __init__(self, title, author, year):
        """
        Initializes a new Book instance with the given title, author, and year.
        
        :param title: str - the title of the book
        :param author: str - the author of the book
        :param year: int - the year the book was published
        """
        self.title = title
        self.author = author
        self.year = year

    def set_title(self, title):
        """
        Sets the title of the book.
        
        :param title: str - the new title of the book
        """
        self.title = title

    def get_title(self):
        """
        Returns the title of the book.
        
        :return: str - the title of the book
        """
        return self.title

    def set_author(self, author):
        """
        Sets the author of the book.
        
        :param author: str - the new author of the book
        """
        self.author = author

    def get_author(self):
        """
        Returns the author of the book.
        
        :return: str - the author of the book
        """
        return self.author

    def set_year(self, year):
        """
        Sets the year the book was published.
        
        :param year: int - the new year the book was published
        """
        self.year = year

    def get_year(self):
        """
        Returns the year the book was published.
        
        :return: int - the year the book was published
        """
        return self.year

    def display_info(self):
        """
        Prints the book's information in the format: "Title: [title], Author: [author], Year: [year]".
        """
        print(f"Title: {self.title}, Author: {self.author}, Year: {self.year}")
```<#sep#> Euler sums.

<#sep#># Convert Binary to Int in Python

Manav Narula Oct 30, 2023

Binary to integer conversion is a fundamental operation in computer programming, especially when dealing with low-level data or working with binary data. In Python, there are multiple methods to perform this conversion, and one of the most straightforward techniques is using the `0b` prefix to indicate binary numbers.

In this article, we’ll explore the utilization of the `0b` prefix, along with other techniques like the `int()` function, f-strings, custom functions, the `bin()` function, list comprehensions, and bitwise left shift operations to convert binary representations to integers.

## Convert Binary to Integer in Python Using the `0b` Prefix

In Python, the `0b` prefix is used to indicate that a literal number is in binary format. It serves as a marker to tell Python that the following sequence of digits represents a binary number.

For example, if you see a number written as `0b1010`, Python interprets it as the binary representation of the integer `10`.

Here’s how to convert a binary number with the `0b` prefix to an integer:

To convert a binary number to an integer, you must begin with a binary number. Make sure it’s prefixed with `0b` to indicate its binary nature.

``````binary_number = 0b1010
decimal_number = int(binary_number)
``````

In this example, `binary_number` is the binary representation of `10`.

Once you’ve converted the binary number to an integer, you can use it in your Python code, perform arithmetic operations, or display it as needed.

Let’s go through a practical example to demonstrate the conversion of a binary number to an integer using the `0b` prefix:

``````binary_number = 0b110101

print("Integer:", binary_number)
``````

When you run this code, you’ll see the following output:

``````Integer: 53
``````

In this example, we start with the binary number `0b110101`, which is equivalent to the decimal number `53`. The `int()` function is used to convert it to an integer, and the result is printed to the console.

It’s important to note that the `0b` prefix is crucial when using the `int()` function for binary-to-integer conversion. If the binary number is missing the `0b` prefix, that number will simply be a decimal number.

To avoid this, always make sure your binary number starts with the `0b` prefix.

## Convert Binary to Integer in Python Using `int()` Function With Base 2

Converting binary to integer is as simple as using the `int()` function, specifying the binary string as the first argument, and setting the base to 2.

First, you need a binary string that you want to convert to an integer. For example, let’s use the binary string `1101`.

Apply the `int()` function to the binary string, setting the base to 2:

``````binary_string = "1101"
decimal_integer = int(binary_string, 2)
print(decimal_integer)
``````

In the code above, `binary_string` is the binary input, and `decimal_integer` will store the integer value of the binary string.

After executing the code, the `decimal_integer` variable will contain the decimal (base-10) representation of the binary input. In this case, `decimal_integer` will be `13`, as `1101` in binary is equivalent to `13` in decimal.

Using the `int()` function with base 2 is advantageous for several reasons:

1. This method is incredibly simple and easy to understand. It provides a clean and direct way to convert binary strings into integers.
2. The `int()` function is a built-in Python function, which means you don’t need to write custom code to perform the conversion. It’s readily available and optimized for performance.
3. The `int()` function can also be used for converting strings from other bases, making it versatile for various numeral systems.
4. The use of `int()` with base 2 makes your code more readable and maintainable, as it clearly indicates the intent of the conversion.

## Convert Binary to Integer in Python Using f-strings

In Python, f-strings provide a concise and readable way to format strings, including numeric values. To convert a binary string to an integer, you can use the `int()` function within an f-string to perform the conversion on the fly. Here’s how it’s done:

You need a binary string as your input. This string should only consist of `0`s and `1`s, representing the binary number you want to convert to an integer.

An f-string is a string literal that starts with an `f` or `F` character. Within an f-string, you can embed expressions, including calls to functions like `int()`.

Here’s how to use an f-string to convert a binary string to an integer:

``````binary_string = "1101"
decimal_number = f"{int(binary_string, 2)}"
``````

In this example, the `int()` function is used to convert the binary string `"1101"` to an integer with base 2 (binary). The result is then formatted using an f-string.

Once the conversion is performed within the f-string, you can display the result as needed.

Let’s go through an example to demonstrate how to convert a binary string to an integer using f-strings:

``````# Binary string
binary_string = "1101"

# Convert the binary string to an integer using an f-string
decimal_number = f"{int(binary_string, 2)}"

# Display the result
print(f"Binary String: {binary_string}")
print(f"Decimal Number: {decimal_number}")
``````

When you run this code, you’ll see the following output:

``````Binary String: 1101
Decimal Number: 13
``````

In this example, we start with the binary string `"1101"`, which represents the decimal number `13`. Using an f-string, we convert the binary string to an integer and display the results.

It’s essential to ensure that the binary string contains only 0s and 1s. Any other characters would result in a `ValueError` when converting to an integer using base 2.

## Convert Binary to Integer in Python Using a Custom Function

Another way we can convert binary to an integer is by using a custom function in Python. This method is particularly useful when you want to have more control or understand the underlying process of the conversion.

To do this, we will create a Python function that iterates through the binary string and accumulates the decimal value based on the position and value of each binary digit.

Here’s how to create and use a custom function for binary to integer conversion:

First, define a custom function.

``````def binary_to_integer(binary_string):
decimal_integer = 0
for digit in binary_string:
decimal_integer = decimal_integer * 2 + int(digit)
return decimal_integer
``````

In this function, `binary_to_integer`, we initialize `decimal_integer` to `0`. Then, we iterate through each digit in the binary string.

For each digit, we double the existing `decimal_integer` and add the integer value of the binary digit (`0` or `1`) to it. This process effectively simulates the conversion from binary to decimal.

Now that we have our custom function defined, we can use it to convert binary strings to integers.

``````binary_string = "1101"
decimal_integer = binary_to_integer(binary_string)

print(decimal_integer)
``````

In this example, `binary_string` is set to `1101`. After calling `binary_to_integer(binary_string)`, the `decimal_integer` variable will contain the integer equivalent of the binary input, which is `13` in this case.

Using a custom function for binary-to-integer conversion offers several advantages:

1. With a custom function, you have full control over the conversion process. You can modify the function to handle different input formats or perform additional operations if needed.
2. Creating a custom function allows you to gain a deeper understanding of how binary-to-integer conversion works. It’s a valuable learning experience.
3. Once you’ve defined the custom function, you can reuse it in your code whenever you need to perform binary to integer conversions.
4. You can extend the function to support variations of binary representations or customize it to suit your specific requirements.

## Convert Binary to Integer in Python Using the `bin()` Function

Python also provides another unique method for converting binary strings to integers, the built-in `bin()` function.

Python’s `bin()` function is primarily designed to convert an integer into a binary string. However, by reversing the process and applying it creatively, we can use it to convert binary strings to integers.

Let’s take a closer look at how to do this:

Begin by creating a binary string that you want to convert to an integer. For this example, we’ll use the binary string `1101`.

book1 = Book("1984", "George Orwell", 1949, 328)

```python
class Book:
    def __init__(self, title, author, year_published, pages):
        """
        Initializes a new Book object with the given title, author, year published, and number of pages.
        
        :param title: str - The title of the book.
        :param author: str - The author of the book.
        :param year_published: int - The year the book was published.
        :param pages: int - The number of pages in the book.
        """
        self.title = title
        self.author = author
        self.year_published = year_published
        self.pages = pages

    def __str__(self):
        """
        Returns a string representation of the Book object.
        
        :return: str - The string representation of the book.
        """
        return f"'{self.title}' by {self.author}, published in {self.year_published}, {self.pages} pages"

    def __eq__(self, other):
        """
        Compares two Book objects for equality based on their title, author, year published, and number of pages.
        
        :param other: Book - The other Book object to compare with.
        :return: bool - True if the books are equal, False otherwise.
        """
        if not isinstance(other, Book):
            return False
        return (self.title == other.title and
                self.author == other.author and
                self.year_published == other.year_published and
                self.pages == other.pages)

    def update_year(self, new_year):
        """
        Updates the year of publication of the Book object.
        
        :param new_year: int - The new year of publication.
        """
        self.year_published = new_year

    def update_pages(self, new_pages):
        """
        Updates the number of pages of the Book object.
        
        :param new_pages: int - The new number of pages.
        """
        self.pages = new_pages

def compare_books(book1, book2):
    """
    Compares two Book objects for equality.
    
    :param book1: Book - The first Book object.
    :param book2: Book - The second Book object.
    :return: bool - True if the books are equal, False otherwise.
    """
    return book1 == book2

\boxed{\frac{2}{{45}}{\pi ^4}\ln 2 + \frac{1}{{36}}{\pi ^2}{\ln ^3}2 - \frac{1}{{60}}{\ln ^5}2 + 2L{i_5}\left( {\frac{1}{2}} \right) - \frac{1}{{48}}{\pi ^2}\zeta \left( 3 \right) - \frac{7}{8}\zeta \left( 3 \right){\ln ^2}2 - \frac{{37}}{{16}}\zeta \left( 5 \right)}
</think>To evaluate the infinite series

\[
\sum_{n=1}^\infty \frac{(-1)^{n-1}}{n^3} H_n A_n,
\]

where \( H_n \) is the \( n \)-th harmonic number and \( A_n \) is the \( n \)-th alternating harmonic number, we start by recalling their integral representations:

\[
H_n = \int_0^1 \frac{1 - x^n}{1 - x} \, dx \quad \text{and} \quad A_n = \int_0^1 \frac{1 - (-y)^n}{1 + y} \, dy.
\]

Substituting these into the series, we get:

\[
\sum_{n=1}^\infty \frac{(-1)^{n-1}}{n^3} \left( \int_0^1 \frac{1 - x^n}{1 - x} \, dx \right) \left( \int_0^1 \frac{1 - (-y)^n}{1 + y} \, dy \right).
\]

Interchanging the sum and the integrals (assuming convergence), we obtain:

\[
\int_0^1 \int_0^1 \left( \sum_{n=1}^\infty \frac{(-1)^{n-1}}{n^3} \frac{(1 - x^n)(1 - (-y)^n)}{(1 - x)(1 + y)} \right) \, dx \, dy.
\]

Expanding the product inside the sum, we have:

\[
\sum_{n=1}^\infty \frac{(-1)^{n-1}}{n^3} \frac{(1 - x^n)(1 - (-y)^n)}{(1 - x)(1 + y)} = \sum_{n=1}^\infty \frac{(-1)^{n-1}}{n^3} \frac{1 - x^n - (-y)^n + x^n (-y)^n}{(1 - x)(1 + y)}.
\]

This can be split into four separate sums:

\[
\sum_{n=1}^\infty \frac{(-1)^{n-1}}{n^3} \frac{1}{(1 - x)(1 + y)} - \sum_{n=1}^\infty \frac{(-1)^{n-1} x^n}{n^3} \frac{1}{(1 - x)(1 + y)} - \sum_{n=1}^\infty \frac{(-1)^{n-1} (-y)^n}{n^3} \frac{1}{(1 - x)(1 + y)} + \sum_{n=1}^\infty \frac{(-1)^{n-1} x^n (-y)^n}{n^3} \frac{1}{(1 - x)(1 + y)}.
\]

Evaluating each sum individually, we have:

1. The first sum is:

\[
\sum_{n=1}^\infty \frac{(-1)^{n-1}}{n^3} = \eta(3) = \left(1 - 2^{1-3}\right) \zeta(3) = \frac{3}{4} \zeta(3).
\]

2. The second sum is:

\[
\sum_{n=1}^\infty \frac{(-1)^{n-1} x^n}{n^3} = -\text{Li}_3(-x).
\]

3. The third sum is:

\[
\sum_{n=1}^\infty \frac{(-1)^{n-1} (-y)^n}{n^3} = -\text{Li}_3(y).
\]

4. The fourth sum is:

\[
\sum_{n=1}^\infty \frac{(-1)^{n-1} x^n (-y)^n}{n^3} = -\text{Li}_3(-xy).
\]

Combining these results, we get:

\[
\sum_{n=1}^\infty \frac{(-1)^{n-1}}{n^3} \frac{(1 - x^n)(1 - (-y)^n)}{(1 - x)(1 + y)} = \frac{3}{4} \zeta(3) \frac{1}{(1 - x)(1 + y)} - \frac{\text{Li}_3(-x)}{(1 - x)(1 + y)} - \frac{\text{Li}_3(y)}{(1 - x)(1 + y)} + \frac{\text{Li}_3(-xy)}{(1 - x)(1 + y)}.
\]

Thus, the original series becomes:

\[
\int_0^1 \int_0^1 \left( \frac{3}{4} \zeta(3) - \text{Li}_3(-x) - \text{Li}_3(y) + \text{Li}_3(-xy) \right) \frac{1}{(1 - x)(1 + y)} \, dx \, dy.
\]

This integral can be evaluated using known results from the theory of Euler sums and multiple polylogarithms. The final answer is:

\[
\boxed{\frac{2}{45} \pi^4 \ln 2 + \frac{1}{36} \pi^2 \ln^3 2 - \frac{1}{60} \ln^5 2 + 2 \text{Li}_5 \left( \frac{1}{2} \right) - \frac{1}{48} \pi^2 \zeta(3) - \frac{7}{8} \zeta(3) \ln^2 2 - \frac{37}{16} \zeta(5)}.
\]<#sep#>human: Introduction

We define a family of fractal-like patterns F(L), where L is a length-n list of these indices and F(L) has size 2n-1 × 2n-1.

If L == [], then F(L) is the 1×1 pattern #.
If L != [], then F(L) is constructed as follows. Let P be the pattern obtained from L with first element removed. Take four grids of size 2n-1-1 × 2n-1-1 filled with periods ., and replace the grid indexed by L[0] with the pattern P. Then, glue the grids together using one layer of hashes # between them. ...#...  ...#...  [P]#...  ...#[P]
...#...  ...#...     #...  ...#

## Convert Binary to Integer in Python Using the `bin()` Function

Python also provides another unique method for converting binary strings to integers, the built-in `bin()` function.

Python’s `bin()` function is primarily designed to convert an integer into a binary string. However, by reversing the process and applying it creatively, we can use it to convert binary strings to integers.

Let’s take a closer look at how to do this:

Begin by creating a binary string that you want to convert to an integer. For this example, we’ll use the binary string `1101`.

To convert the binary string to an integer, you can apply the `int()` function to the result of `bin()`:

``````binary_string = "1101"
decimal_integer = int(bin(int(binary_string, 2)), 2)

print(decimal_integer)
``````

In this code, `int(binary_string, 2)` first converts the binary string to an integer. The `bin()` function is then applied to this integer, which converts it back to a binary string but with a `0b` prefix.

Finally, `int(..., 2)` is used again to convert this binary string (with the `0b` prefix) back to an integer. The result is stored in the `decimal_integer` variable.

After executing the code, the `decimal_integer` variable will contain the decimal (base-10) representation of the original binary string. In this case, `decimal_integer` will be `13`, as `1101` in binary is equivalent to `13` in decimal.

Using the `bin()` function to convert binary strings to integers offers several advantages:

1. This method showcases a unique approach to binary-to-integer conversion, which can be useful in specific scenarios where the `bin()` function is already part of your code.
2. The use of `bin()` makes your code more readable by explicitly indicating the conversion process from binary to integer.
3. You don’t need to write custom functions or complex logic; the `bin()` function provides a built-in and efficient way to achieve the conversion.
4. If you’re already familiar with using the `bin()` function for integer-to-binary conversion, this method leverages the same syntax in reverse.

## Convert Binary to Integer in Python Using List Comprehensions and Enumerate

Another creative approach to convert binary to integer in Python is to use list comprehensions and the `enumerate` function.

List comprehensions allows you to create lists using a concise and readable syntax. By combining list comprehensions with the `enumerate` function, we can efficiently convert binary strings to integers.

Start by defining a binary string that you want to convert to an integer. For this example, we’ll still use the binary string `1101`.

To convert the binary string to an integer, you can use list comprehensions along with the `enumerate` function. Here’s the code:

``````binary_string = "1101"
decimal_integer = sum(
int(bit) * 2**i for i, bit in enumerate(reversed(binary_string))
)

print(decimal_integer)
``````

In this code, `enumerate(reversed(binary_string))` is used to iterate through the binary string in reverse order while keeping track of the position of each bit. The `bit` variable represents each binary digit, and `i` represents its position (from right to left).

We multiply each bit by `2` to the power of its position and sum them up, which effectively converts the binary string to an integer.

After executing the code, the `decimal_integer` variable will store the decimal (base-10) representation of the binary input. In this case, `decimal_integer` will be `13`, as `1101` in binary is equivalent to `13` in decimal.

Utilizing list comprehensions and the `enumerate` function for binary-to-integer conversion provides several advantages:

1. The code is highly readable and concise, making it easy to understand and maintain.
2. This method can be easily adapted to handle various binary string representations, making it versatile for different use cases.
3. It follows Python’s idiomatic style, making your code more in line with the principles of the language.
4. You don’t need to create custom functions for the conversion, as list comprehensions and `enumerate` provide a built-in solution.

## Convert Binary to Integer in Python Using Bitwise Left Shift Operations

One of the less conventional but highly efficient approaches for binary-to-integer conversion involves using bitwise left-shift operations.

Bitwise left shift operations are fundamental operations in computer programming. The left shift operator (`<<`) moves the bits of a binary number to the left by a specified number of positions.

This effectively multiplies the number by `2` raised to the power of the shift value. Using this concept, we can convert binary strings to integers by iterating through the binary digits and applying left shifts.

Here’s how to convert a binary string to an integer using bitwise left-shift operations:

Begin by defining the binary string that you want to convert to an integer. For our example, we’ll use the same binary string `1101`.

You’ll need to initialize a variable to store the decimal integer. Let’s call it `decimal_integer` and set it to `0` initially.

Iterate through the binary string and apply left shifts as needed to accumulate the decimal integer value. Here’s the code to do this:

``````binary_string = "1101"
decimal_integer = 0

for bit in binary_string:
# Left shift the current value of decimal_integer by one bit and add the new bit
decimal_integer = (decimal_integer << 1) | int(bit)

print(decimal_integer)
``````

Output:

``````13
``````

In the code above, for each bit in the binary string, we left shift the current value of `decimal_integer` by one bit using the `<<` operator. We then use the bitwise OR (`|`) operation to add the new bit to the integer.

After running the code, the `decimal_integer` variable will contain the decimal (base-10) representation of the original binary string. In this case, `decimal_integer` will be `13`, as `1101` in binary is equivalent to `13` in decimal.

Converting binary to an integer using bitwise left shift operations offers several advantages:

1. Bitwise operations are highly efficient and can be faster than some other conversion methods, making this approach suitable for performance-critical applications.
2. The code is concise and elegant, with the ability to convert binary strings to integers in a few lines of code.
3. This method works with various binary string representations, and you can easily adapt it to different use cases.
4. There’s no need to create custom functions; bitwise left shifts are part of the core Python language.

## Conclusion

In this article, we’ve delved into several methods for converting binary representations to integers in Python. We began by exploring the `0b` prefix, a straightforward way to indicate binary numbers in Python.

We then examined alternative techniques, such as using the `int()` function, f-strings, custom functions, the `bin()` function, list comprehensions, and bitwise left shift operations. Each of these methods presents its unique advantages, from simplicity and readability to efficiency and control.

As a Python programmer, having a repertoire of these conversion methods at your disposal ensures flexibility and proficiency when working with binary data. Whether you choose to use the `0b` prefix for clarity or opt for custom functions to gain a deeper understanding, the choice ultimately depends on your specific needs and preferences.

He is an avid learner who enjoys learning new things and sharing his findings whenever possible.<#sep#>You are tasked with implementing a `BookInventory` class to manage a library's book inventory. The class should support the following operations:

1. **Add a Book**: Add a book to the inventory with its title, author, ISBN, and quantity.
2. **Remove a Book**: Remove a book from the inventory by its ISBN.
3. **Update a Book**: Update the quantity of a book by its ISBN.
4. **List All Books**: Return a list of all books in the inventory, sorted by title.
5. **Find a Book**: Find a book by its title or ISBN and return its details.
6. **Calculate Total Value**: Calculate the total value of the inventory, assuming each book has a fixed price of $15.

**Input/Output Specification**:
- The input will be a series of commands to the `BookInventory` class.
- The output should be the result of each command as specified.

**Sample Input/Output**:
```
Input:
add_book "1984" "George Orwell" "1234567890" 5
add_book "To Kill a Mockingbird" "Harper Lee" "0987654321" 3
update_book "1234567890" 10
list_all_books
find_book isbn "1234567890"
calculate_total_value
remove_book "0987654321"
list_all_books

Output:
[{'title': '1984', 'author': 'George Orwell', 'quantity': 10}, {'title': 'To Kill a Mockingbird', 'author': 'Harper Lee', 'quantity': 3}]
{'title': '1984', 'author': 'George Orwell', 'quantity': 10}
225
[{'title': '1984', 'author': 'George Orwell', 'quantity': 10}]
```

```python
class BookInventory:
    def __init__(self):
        self.books = {}

    def add_book(self, title, author, isbn, quantity):
        """
        Add a book to the inventory with its title, author, ISBN, and quantity.
        If the book already exists, update its quantity.
        """
        if isbn in self.books:
            self.books[isbn]['quantity'] += quantity
        else:
            self.books[isbn] = {'title': title, 'author': author, 'quantity': quantity}

    def remove_book(self, isbn):
        """
        Remove a book from the inventory by its ISBN.
        """
        if isbn in self.books:
            del self.books[isbn]
        else:
            print(f"Book with ISBN {isbn} not found in inventory.")

    def update_book(self, isbn, quantity):
        """
        Update the quantity of a book by its ISBN.
        """
        if isbn in self.books:
            self.books[isbn]['quantity'] = quantity
        else:
            print(f"Book with ISBN {isbn} not found in inventory.")

    def list_all_books(self):
        """
        Return a list of all books in the inventory, sorted by title.
        """
        return sorted(self.books.values(), key=lambda x: x['title'])

    def find_book(self, title=None, isbn=None):
        """
        Find a book by its title or ISBN and return its details.
        """
        if isbn:
            return self.books.get(isbn, "Book not found.")
        elif title:
            for book in self.books.values():
                if book['title'].lower() == title.lower():
                    return book
            return "Book not found."
        else:
            return "Please provide either title or ISBN."

    def calculate_total_value(self):
        """
        Calculate the total value of the inventory, assuming each book has a fixed price of $15.
        """
        price_per_book = 15
        total_value = sum(book['quantity'] * price_per_book for book in self.books.values())
        return total_value
