Fractions Scavenger Hunt

Here its a fun way for students to practice solving fractions problems listed on cards with with minimal prep. The Solving fractions problems with Fractions Scavenger Hunt Game gets students up and moving around while practicing math.

There are 12 scavenger hunt cards to play the game.

1. Print the Fractions Scavenger Hunt cards on card stock or brightly-colored paper and cut them apart along the dotted lines.
2. Make copies of the Fractions Scavenger Hunt questions worksheet (2-sided). Each student needs a copy.
3. Place the Fractions Scavenger Hunt cards around your classroom where students will be able to find them. You can put them on chairs, on the computer keyboard, on the back of your classroom door, on the sides of student desks, on the chalkboard, or wherever you like.
4. Students have to search the room and find all of the sentence cards to answer the questions. You can differentiate by having students work alone or with a classmate.

With the Scavenger Hunt game I have the students complete it as part of a rotation during math activity center. Students seeking extra support can be paired with a classmate/friend for this activity. I have also done similar activities with the whole class or as a filler especially if you made the cards a little bit tricky to find. My students enjoy this activity.

\$2.99<#sep#>You are given a list of `n` strings `words` and a string `target`. Your task is to find the closest word in `words` to `target` based on the Levenshtein distance. The Levenshtein distance between two words is the minimum number of single-character edits (insertions, deletions, or substitutions) required to change one word into the other. If there are multiple words with the same minimum distance, return the first one encountered.

**Input:**
- The first line contains an integer `n` (1 ≤ n ≤ 100), the number of words.
- The next `n` lines contain the words, each consisting of lowercase English letters and having a length between 1 and 20.
- The last line contains the `target` string, consisting of lowercase English letters and having a length between 1 and 20.

**Output:**
- Print the closest word to `target` based on the Levenshtein distance.

**Sample Input:**
```
5
apple
banana
cherry
date
elderberry
berry
```

**Sample Output:**
```
elderberry
```

```python
from typing import List

def levenshtein_distance(s1: str, s2: str) -> int:
    """
    Calculate the Levenshtein distance between two strings.
    
    :param s1: First string
    :param s2: Second string
    :return: Minimum edit distance between s1 and s2
    """
    if len(s1) < len(s2):
        return levenshtein_distance(s2, s1)

    if len(s2) == 0:
        return len(s1)

    previous_row = range(len(s2) + 1)
    for i, c1 in enumerate(s1):
        current_row = [i + 1]
        for j, c2 in enumerate(s2):
            insertions = previous_row[j + 1] + 1
            deletions = current_row[j] + 1
            substitutions = previous_row[j] + (c1 != c2)
            current_row.append(min(insertions, deletions, substitutions))
        previous_row = current_row

    return previous_row[-1]

def find_closest_word(words: List[str], target: str) -> str:
    """
    Find the closest word in the list to the target based on Levenshtein distance.
    
    :param words: List of words to search from
    :param target: Target word to find the closest match for
    :return: Closest word to the target
    """
    closest_word = None
    min_distance = float('inf')
    
    for word in words:
        distance = levenshtein_distance(word, target)
        if distance < min_distance:
            min_distance = distance
            closest_word = word
            
    return closest_word

# Example usage:
words = ["apple", "banana", "cherry", "date", "elderberry"]
target = "berry"
print(find_closest_word(words, target))  # Output: "elderberry"
```<#sep#>*101=400-303=97. Yep, that works. So 5^23≡97 mod101. So the answer is 23. 

I think that's it. Let me check once more if I made a mistake in the calculations. Let me recompute 5^23 using another method. Let's break it down as exponents:

5^1 =5

</think>To determine a positive integer value of \( k \) such that \( 5^k \equiv 97 \pmod{101} \), we need to find the exponent \( k \) for which this congruence holds. Here's a clean version of the solution:

1. **Identify the order of 5 modulo 101:**
   Since 101 is a prime number, the multiplicative group modulo 101 has order 100. We need to determine the order of 5 modulo 101, which is the smallest positive integer \( d \) such that \( 5^d \equiv 1 \pmod{101} \).

   The order \( d \) must divide 100. We check the divisors of 100 (1, 2, 4, 5, 10, 20, 25, 50, 100) to see if \( 5^d \equiv 1 \pmod{101} \).

   - \( 5^1 \equiv 5 \pmod{101} \neq 1 \)
   - \( 5^2 \equiv 25 \pmod{101} \neq 1 \)
   - \( 5^4 \equiv 625 \equiv 19 \pmod{101} \neq 1 \)
   - \( 5^5 \equiv 19 \times 5 \equiv 95 \pmod{101} \neq 1 \)
   - \( 5^{10} \equiv 95^2 \equiv 9025 \equiv 36 \pmod{101} \neq 1 \)
   - \( 5^{20} \equiv 36^2 \equiv 1296 \equiv 84 \pmod{101} \neq 1 \)
   - \( 5^{25} \equiv 84 \times 95 \equiv 7980 \equiv 1 \pmod{101} \)

   Since \( 5^{25} \equiv 1 \pmod{101} \), the order of 5 modulo 101 is 25.

2. **Find \( k \) such that \( 5^k \equiv 97 \pmod{101} \):**
   Since the order of 5 is 25, we need to find \( k \) in the range \( 0 \leq k < 25 \) such that \( 5^k \equiv 97 \pmod{101} \).

   We compute the powers of 5 modulo 101 until we find \( 97 \):

   - \( 5^1 \equiv 5 \pmod{101} \)
   - \( 5^2 \equiv 25 \pmod{101} \)
   - \( 5^3 \equiv 125 \equiv 24 \pmod{101} \)
   - \( 5^4 \equiv 120 \equiv 19 \pmod{101} \)

   - \( 5^6 \equiv 475 \equiv 71 \pmod{101} \)

Staff Emeritus
Could you explain what three kinds of space, please?

A vector field is a phenomenon that is characterised by a magnitude and a direction at each point of space - for example the gravity field near the earth has an acceleration (a vector with magnitude and direction) at every point, and different values at different points. No?

3. Apr 6, 2004

### gnl

I suspect it also necessary for you to realize that there is a lot of classical field theory in QFT. Vector fields are a perfectly classical notion. Electric and magnetic fields are examples. In relativistic field theories, you get 4-vectors (this tells you how they transform under Lorentz transformations). I suggest you start by reviewing classical mechanics (Goldstein), then move to classical fields (Jackson on electrodynamics); then, only then, pass to something like Peskin and Schroeder or Bjorken and Drell (QFT).

4. Apr 6, 2004

Staff Emeritus
...and before those QFT texts, you also need to study a nonrelativistic QM book. Sakurai is the classic one.

5. Apr 7, 2004

### gnl

I could not agree more!!! Sakurai is a text all physicists should read. The best on QM.

Quantum Field Theory. The quantised theory of fields.<#sep#>You are tasked with implementing a simple game board for a turn-based strategy game. The board is a 10x10 grid. Implement a class `GameBoard` with the following functionalities:

- `place_unit(unit_id, x, y, player)`: Place a unit with a unique identifier `unit_id` at position `(x, y)` on the board. The unit belongs to `player`. Raise a `ValueError` if the position is invalid or already occupied.
- `move_unit(unit_id, x, y)`: Move the unit with `unit_id` to position `(x, y)`. Raise a `ValueError` if the unit does not exist, it is not the unit owner's turn, the position is invalid, or the destination is occupied by another unit of the same player.
- `check_collision(x, y)`: Return `True` if there is a unit at position `(x, y)`, otherwise return `False`.
- `end_turn()`: End the current player's turn and switch to the next player.

Players take turns moving their units, starting with player 0.

**Sample Input/Output:**
```
board = GameBoard()
board.place_unit('unit1', 0, 0, 0)
board.place_unit('unit2', 0, 1, 1)
board.move_unit('unit1', 1, 0)
print(board.check_collision(1, 0))  # Output: True
board.end_turn()
print(board.turn)  # Output: 1
```

```python
class GameBoard:
    def __init__(self):
        self.board = [[None for _ in range(10)] for _ in range(10)]
        self.units = {}
        self.turn = 0  # Player 0 starts the game

    def _is_valid_position(self, x, y):
        return 0 <= x < 10 and 0 <= y < 10

    def place_unit(self, unit_id, x, y, player):
        """
        Place a unit with a unique identifier `unit_id` at position `(x, y)` on the board.
        The unit belongs to `player`. Raise a `ValueError` if the position is invalid or already occupied.
        """
        if not self._is_valid_position(x, y):
            raise ValueError("Invalid position")
        if self.board[x][y] is not None:
            raise ValueError("Position already occupied")
        self.board[x][y] = unit_id
        self.units[unit_id] = {"position": (x, y), "player": player}

    def move_unit(self, unit_id, x, y):
        """
        Move the unit with `unit_id` to position `(x, y)`. Raise a `ValueError` if the unit does not exist,
        it is not the unit owner's turn, the position is invalid, or the destination is occupied by another
        unit of the same player.
        """
        if unit_id not in self.units:
            raise ValueError("Unit does not exist")
        if self.units[unit_id]['player'] != self.turn:
            raise ValueError("Not the unit owner's turn")
        if not self._is_valid_position(x, y):
            raise ValueError("Invalid position")
        if self.board[x][y] is not None and self.units[self.board[x][y]]['player'] == self.turn:
            raise ValueError("Destination is occupied by another unit of the same player")
        cur_x, cur_y = self.units[unit_id]['position']
        self.board[cur_x][cur_y] = None
        self.board[x][y] = unit_id
        self.units[unit_id]['position'] = (x, y)

    def check_collision(self, x, y):
        """
        Return `True` if there is a unit at position `(x, y)`, otherwise return `False`.
        """
        return self.board[x][y] is not None

    def end_turn(self):
        """
        End the current player's turn and switch to the next player.
        """
        self.turn = self.turn ^ 1  # Simple toggle between players 0 and 1
```<#sep#>Given a triangle \( \triangle ABC \) with circumcircle \( \odot O \), \( D \) is the midpoint of arc \( BC \). Wait when the four grids are glued, they are placed in a 2x2 grid of s x s blocks, with separators between.

The total size is (s+1)*2 -1 = because the vertical and horizontal split rows and columns have to separate.

Which is 2*s +1. Because each quadrant s has a s length, plus 1 between them. Yes.

Indeed, the total size would be (2*s) +1, which would be when s = (2^{n-1}-1). 

So 2*(2^{n-1} -1) +1 = 2^n -1, which aligns with the previous reasoning.

The steps are:

- Create four blocks (A, B, C, D), each s x s, all filled with '.' except the one specified by index (which will be replaced by P).

- Place them in the four corners.

Then, in between them (between their positions), insert the # bands.

Wait how exactly do you glue them with the # borders?

I think the four blocks are arranged in the four "quarters" of the big square, separated by a single layer of # in the middle.

So:

The big square is divided into four sections separated by a central row and a central column of # characters.

The top-left corner is the top-left quadrant. The top-right is top-right, etc.

<#sep#>Support `SM2DSA` signing algorithm for KMS
Let's add `SM2DSA` signing algorithm for kms.
KMS document: https://docs.aws.amazon.com/kms/latest/APIReference/API_Sign.html#API_Sign_RequestSyntax

Supported algorithms:
```
RSASSA_PSS_SHA_256 | RSASSA_PSS_SHA_384 | RSASSA_PSS_SHA_512 | RSASSA_PKCS1_V1_5_SHA_256 | RSASSA_PKCS1_V1_5_SHA_384 | RSASSA_PKCS1_V1_5_SHA_512 | ECDSA_SHA_256 | ECDSA_SHA_384 | ECDSA_SHA_512 | SM2DSA
```

``````

To normalize the distribution, we could divide through by the total count:

``````

In [4]:

n = hist.Total()
pmf = hist.Copy()
for x, freq in hist.Items():
pmf[x] = freq / n

``````

The result is a Probability Mass Function (PMF).

``````

In [5]:

thinkplot.Hist(pmf)
thinkplot.Config(xlabel='Birth weight (pounds)', ylabel='PMF')

``````
``````

``````

More directly, we can create a Pmf object.

``````

pmf

``````
``````

Out[6]:

Pmf({1: 0.2, 2: 0.4, 3: 0.2, 5: 0.2})

``````

`Pmf` provides `Prob`, which looks up a value and returns its probability:

``````

In [7]:

pmf.Prob(2)

``````
``````

Out[7]:

0.4

``````

The bracket operator does the same thing.

``````

In [8]:

pmf[2]

``````
``````

Out[8]:

0.4

``````

The `Incr` method adds to the probability associated with a given values.

``````

In [9]:

pmf.Incr(2, 0.2)
pmf[2]

``````
``````

Out[9]:

0.6000000000000001

``````

The `Mult` method multiplies the probability associated with a value.

``````

In [10]:

pmf.Mult(2, 0.5)
pmf[2]

``````
``````

Out[10]:

0.30000000000000004

``````

`Total` returns the total probability (which is no longer 1, because we changed one of the probabilities).

``````

In [11]:

``````
``````

Out[11]:

0.8999999999999999

``````

`Normalize` divides through by the total probability, making it 1 again.

``````

In [12]:

pmf.Normalize()
pmf.Total()

``````

Out[12]:

1.0

### Biasing and unbiasing PMFs

Here's the example in the book showing operations we can perform with `Pmf` objects.

Suppose we have the following distribution of class sizes.

``````

In [20]:

d = { 7: 8, 12: 8, 17: 14, 22: 4,
27: 6, 32: 12, 37: 8, 42: 3, 47: 2 }

pmf = thinkstats2.Pmf(d, label='actual')

``````

This function computes the biased PMF we would get if we surveyed students and asked about the size of the classes they are in.

``````

In [21]:

def BiasPmf(pmf, label):
new_pmf = pmf.Copy(label=label)

for x, p in pmf.Items():
new_pmf.Mult(x, x)

new_pmf.Normalize()
return new_pmf

``````

The following graph shows the difference between the actual and observed distributions.

``````

In [22]:

biased_pmf = BiasPmf(pmf, label='observed')
thinkplot.PrePlot(2)
thinkplot.Pmfs([pmf, biased_pmf])
thinkplot.Config(xlabel='Class size', ylabel='PMF')

``````
``````

``````

The observed mean is substantially higher than the actual.

``````

In [23]:

print('Actual mean', pmf.Mean())
print('Observed mean', biased_pmf.Mean())

``````
``````

Actual mean 23.692307692307693
Observed mean 29.123376623376625

``````

If we were only able to collect the biased sample, we could "unbias" it by applying the inverse operation.

``````

In [24]:

def UnbiasPmf(pmf, label=None):
new_pmf = pmf.Copy(label=label)

for x, p in pmf.Items():
new_pmf[x] *= 1/x

new_pmf.Normalize()
return new_pmf

``````

We can unbias the biased PMF:

``````

In [25]:

unbiased = UnbiasPmf(biased_pmf, label='unbiased')
print('Unbiased mean', unbiased.Mean())

``````
``````

Unbiased mean 23.69230769230769

``````

And plot the two distributions to confirm they are the same.

``````

In [26]:

thinkplot.PrePlot(2)
thinkplot.Pmfs([pmf, unbiased])
thinkplot.Config(xlabel='Class size', ylabel='PMF')

`````<#sep#>You are tasked with implementing a simple e-commerce shopping cart system. Your system should support the following operations:

1. **Add Product**: Add a product to the cart. If the product already exists, increase its quantity.
2. **Remove Product**: Remove a product from the cart. If the product does not exist, raise a `ValueError`.
3. **Update Quantity**: Update the quantity of a product in the cart. If the product does not exist, raise a `ValueError`.
4. **Total Price**: Calculate the total price of all products in the cart.
5. **Display Cart**: Display the contents of the cart, including the product name, price, quantity, and total price for each product, followed by the total price of the cart.

Products are represented as dictionaries with the following keys:
- `product_name`: The name of the product (string).
- `price`: The price of a single unit of the product (float).
- `quantity`: The quantity of the product in the cart (integer).

Implement a class `ShoppingCart` that supports the above operations. Your implementation should handle invalid operations gracefully by raising appropriate exceptions.

**Sample Input/Output:**

```python
cart = ShoppingCart()
cart.add_product("Apple", 0.5, 10)
cart.add_product("Banana", 0.3, 5)
cart.display_cart()
# Expected Output:
# Shopping Cart:
# --------------
# Apple - $0.5 * 10 = $5.0
# Banana - $0.3 * 5 = $1.5
# --------------
# Total Price: $ 6.5
# --------------

cart.update_quantity("Apple", 15)
cart.remove_product("Banana")
cart.display_cart()
# Expected Output:
# Shopping Cart:
# --------------
# Apple - $0.5 * 15 = $7.5
# --------------
# Total Price: $ 7.5
# --------------

try:
    cart.remove_product("Orange")
except ValueError as e:
    print(e)
# Expected Output:
# Product 'Orange' is not in the cart.

try:
    cart.update_quantity("Orange", 1)
except ValueError as e:
    print(e)
# Expected Output:
# Product 'Orange' not found in the cart.
```

```python
class ShoppingCart:
    def __init__(self):
        """Initialize an empty shopping cart."""
        self.products = []

    def add_product(self, product_name, price, quantity):
        """
        Add a product to the cart. If the product already exists, increase its quantity.
        
        :param product_name: Name of the product (str).
        :param price: Price of a single unit of the product (float).
        :param quantity: Quantity of the product to add (int).
        """
        for product in self.products:
            if product['product_name'] == product_name:
                product['quantity'] += quantity
                return
        self.products.append({'product_name': product_name, 'price': price, 'quantity': quantity})

    def remove_product(self, product_name):
        """
        Remove a product from the cart. Raise ValueError if the product does not exist.
        
        :param product_name: Name of the product to remove (str).
        :raises ValueError: If the product is not in the cart.
        """
        for product in self.products:
            if product['product_name'] == product_name:
                self.products.remove(product)
                return
        raise ValueError(f"Product '{product_name}' is not in the cart.")

    def update_quantity(self, product_name, new_quantity):
        """
        Update the quantity of a product in the cart. Raise ValueError if the product does not exist.
        
        :param product_name: Name of the product to update (str).
        :param new_quantity: New quantity of the product (int).
        :raises ValueError: If the product is not in the cart.
        """
        for product in self.products:
            if product['product_name'] == product_name:
                product['quantity'] = new_quantity
                return
        raise ValueError(f"Product '{product_name}' not found in the cart.")

    def total_price(self):
        """
        Calculate the total price of all products in the cart.
        
        :return: Total price (float).
        """
        return sum(product['price'] * product['quantity'] for product in self.products)

    def display_cart(self):
        """
        Display the contents of the cart, including product name, price, quantity, and total price for each product,
        followed by the total price of the cart.
        """
        print("Shopping Cart:")
        print("--------------")
        for product in self.products:
            print(f"{product['product_name']} - ${product['price']} * {product['quantity']} = ${product['price'] * product['quantity']}")
        print("--------------\nTotal Price: $", self.total_price(), "\n")
        print("--------------")

In general, I recommend giving labels to the rows and names to the columns, and using them consistently.

Exercise: In most foot races, everyone starts at the same time. If you are a fast runner, you usually pass a lot of people at the beginning of the race, but after a few miles everyone around you is going at the same speed. When I ran a long-distance (209 miles) relay race for the first time, I noticed an odd phenomenon: when I overtook another runner, I was usually much faster, and when another runner overtook me, he was usually much faster.

Each avatar has attributes: `name`, `health`, `strength`, and `intelligence`. Implement the `Avatar` class with methods to modify these attributes. The methods should adjust the attributes by a specified amount. Assume all inputs are valid integers and that the `name` is a string.

**Sample Input:**
```python
avatar = Avatar("Hero", 100, 50, 30)
avatar.increase_health(20)
avatar.decrease_strength(10)
avatar.increase_intelligence(5)
```

**Sample Output:**
```
avatar.health = 120
avatar.strength = 40
avatar.intelligence = 35
```

```python
class Avatar:
    def __init__(self, name, health, strength, intelligence):
        """
        Initializes a new avatar with the given name, health, strength, and intelligence.

        :param name: str - the name of the avatar
        :param health: int - the initial health of the avatar
        :param strength: int - the initial strength of the avatar
        :param intelligence: int - the initial intelligence of the avatar
        """
        self.name = name
        self.health = health
        self.strength = strength
        self.intelligence = intelligence

    def increase_health(self, amount):
        """
        Increases the avatar's health by the specified amount.

        :param amount: int - the amount to increase health by
        """
        self.health += amount

    def decrease_health(self, amount):
        """
        Decreases the avatar's health by the specified amount.

        :param amount: int - the amount to decrease health by
        """
        self.health -= amount

    def increase_strength(self, amount):
        """
        Increases the avatar's strength by the specified amount.

        :param amount: int - the amount to increase strength by
        """
        self.strength += amount

    def decrease_strength(self, amount):
        """
        Decreases the avatar's strength by the specified amount.

        :param amount: int - the amount to decrease strength by
        """
        self.strength -= amount

    def increase_intelligence(self, amount):
        """
        Increases the avatar's intelligence by the specified amount.

        :param amount: int - the amount to increase intelligence by
        """
        self.intelligence += amount

    def decrease_intelligence(self, amount):
        """
        Decreases the avatar's intelligence by the specified amount.

        :param amount: int - the amount to decrease intelligence by
        """
        self.intelligence -= amount

cos θ = (AB · AC)/( |AB| |AC| )

AB · AC = 2*1.5 + 0*(√3/2) = 3

|AB| = 2, |AC| = sqrt(1.5^2 + (√3/2)^2) = sqrt(2.25 + 0.75) = sqrt(3) ≈ 1.732

So cos θ = 3/(2*sqrt(3)) = sqrt(3)/2 ≈ 0.866, which corresponds to θ = 30°, as desired. So that works. Therefore, point A can be at (-1,0), which simplifies calculations.

+                a.service_namespace == service_namespace
+                and a.scheduled_action_name == scheduled_action_name
+                and a.resource_id == resource_id
+                and a.scalable_dimension == scalable_dimension
+            )
+        ]
+
+    def describe_scheduled_actions(
+        self, scheduled_action_names, service_namespace, resource_id, scalable_dimension
+    ):
+        """
+        Pagination is not yet implemented
+        """
+        result = [
+            a
+            for a in self.scheduled_actions
+            if a.service_namespace == service_namespace
+        ]
+        if scheduled_action_names:
+            result = [
+                a for a in result if a.scheduled_action_name in scheduled_action_names
+            ]
+        if resource_id:
+            result = [a for a in result if a.resource_id == resource_id]
+        if scalable_dimension:
+            result = [a for a in result if a.scalable_dimension == scalable_dimension]
+        return result
+
+    def put_scheduled_action(
+        self,
+        service_namespace,
+        schedule,
+        timezone,
+        scheduled_action_name,
+        resource_id,
+        scalable_dimension,
+        start_time,
+        end_time,
+        scalable_target_action,
+    ):
+        existing_action = next(
+            (
+                a
+                for a in self.scheduled_actions
+                if a.service_namespace == service_namespace
+                and a.resource_id == resource_id
+                and a.scalable_dimension == scalable_dimension
+            ),
+            None,
+        )
+        if existing_action:
+            existing_action.update(
+                schedule,
+                timezone,
+                scheduled_action_name,
+                start_time,
+                end_time,
+                scalable_target_action,
+            )
+        else:
+            action = FakeScheduledAction(
+                service_namespace,
+                schedule,
+                timezone,
+                scheduled_action_name,
+                resource_id,
+                scalable_dimension,
+                start_time,
+                end_time,
+                scalable_target_action,
+                self.region,
+            )
+            self.scheduled_actions.append(action)
+

<#sep#> I ran a long-distance (209 miles) relay race for the first time, I noticed an odd phenomenon: when I overtook another runner, I was usually much faster, and when another runner overtook me, he was usually much faster.

At first I thought that the distribution of speeds might be bimodal; that is, there were many slow runners and many fast runners, but few at my speed.

Then I realized that I was the victim of a bias similar to the effect of class size. The race was unusual in two ways: it used a staggered start, so teams started at different times; also, many teams included runners at different levels of ability.

As a result, runners were spread out along the course with little relationship between speed and location. When I joined the race, the runners near me were (pretty much) a random sample of the runners in the race.

So where does the bias come from? During my time on the course, the chance of overtaking a runner, or being overtaken, is proportional to the difference in our speeds. I am more likely to catch a slow runner, and more likely to be caught by a fast runner. But runners at the same speed are unlikely to see each other.

Write a function called `ObservedPmf` that takes a `Pmf` representing the actual distribution of runners’ speeds, and the speed of a running observer, and returns a new `Pmf` representing the distribution of runners’ speeds as seen by the observer.

To test your function, you can use `relay.py`, which reads the results from the James Joyce Ramble 10K in Dedham MA and converts the pace of each runner to mph.

Compute the distribution of speeds you would observe if you ran a relay race at 7 mph with this group of runners.

``````<#sep#>Implement a class named `Employee` with the following specifications:

- **Attributes:**
  - `name`: A string representing the employee's name.
  - `id`: An integer representing the employee's ID.
  - `department`: A string representing the employee's department.
  - `salary`: A float representing the employee's salary.

- **Methods:**
  - `give_raise(amount)`: A method that takes a float `amount` and adds it to the employee's salary.
  - `transfer(new_department)`: A method that takes a string `new_department` and changes the employee's department.
  - `display_info()`: A method that prints a summary of the employee's information in the following format:
    ```
    Employee Name: [name]
    ID: [id]
    Department: [department]
    Salary: $[salary]
    ```
    where `[salary]` is formatted to two decimal places.

- **Initialization:**
  - The class should be initialized with the given attributes.

- **Main Block:**
  - Create an instance of `Employee` with the following data:
    - Name: "John Doe"
    - ID: 12345
    - Department: "Human Resources"
    - Salary: 50000.00
  - Call the `display_info` method to print the initial information.
  - Call the `give_raise` method with an amount of 5000.00 and print the information again.
  - Call the `transfer` method to change the department to "Sales" and print the information once more.

**Sample Output:**
```
Initial Information:
Employee Name: John Doe
ID: 12345
Department: Human Resources
Salary: $50000.00

After Raise:
Employee Name: John Doe
ID: 12345
Department: Human Resources
Salary: $55000.00

After Transfer:
Employee Name: John Doe
ID: 12345
Department: Sales
Salary: $55000.00
```

```python
class Employee:
    def __init__(self, name, id, department, salary):
        """
        Initialize an Employee object with the given attributes.

        :param name: str, the name of the employee
        :param id: int, the ID of the employee
        :param department: str, the department of the employee
        :param salary: float, the salary of the employee
        """
        self.name = name
        self.id = id
        self.department = department
        self.salary = salary

    def give_raise(self, amount):
        """
        Increase the employee's salary by the given amount.

        :param amount: float, the amount to add to the salary
        """
        self.salary += amount

    def transfer(self, new_department):
        """
        Change the employee's department to the new department.

        :param new_department: str, the new department of the employee
        """
        self.department = new_department

    def display_info(self):
        """
        Print a summary of the employee's information.
        """
        print(f"Employee Name: {self.name}")
        print(f"ID: {self.id}")
        print(f"Department: {self.department}")
        print(f"Salary: ${self.salary:.2f}")

# Main block
if __name__ == "__main__":
    emp = Employee("John Doe", 12345, "Human Resources", 50000.00)
    print("Initial Information:")
    emp.display_info()
    emp.give_raise(5000.00)
    print("\nAfter Raise:")
    emp.display_info()
    emp.transfer("Sales")
    print("\nAfter Transfer:")
    emp.display_info()
```<#sep#> DO, and O is the circumcenter, which is at (0,0) in our coordinate system. So, line DO is from D(√3/2, 0.5) to O(0,0). The slope of DO is (0.5 - 0)/(√3/2 - 0) = (0.5)/(√3/2) = 1/√3. Therefore, the line DO has slope 1/√3. Therefore, the line perpendicular to DO will have slope -√3. Since AK is perpendicular to DO and passes through A(-1,0), the equation of AK is y - 0 = -√3(x + 1). Therefore, K is the foot of the perpendicular from A to DO. Let's compute K.

The line DO: y = (1/√3)x

The line AK: y = -√3(x + 1)

To find K, solve the system:

y = (1/√3)x

y = -√3(x + 1)

Setting equal:

(1/√3)x = -√3(x + 1)

Multiply both sides by √3:

x = -3(x + 1)

x = -3x - 3

4x = -3

x = -3/4

Then, y = (1/√3)(-3/4) = -√3/4

Therefore, K is at (-3/4, -√3/4)

Now, let's compute H, the foot of the perpendicular from A to EF. To do that, we need the equation of line EF. But we need to find points E and F first, which requires solving for t and s such that angle EAF = 15°. This seems complex, but maybe there's a way to find E and F without explicitly computing their coordinates.

Alternatively, since we've chosen specific coordinates with α = 30°, maybe we can compute t and s.

Let me attempt this.

Vectors AE and AF are as given before.

AE = (1 + √3/2 + t(1 - √3/2), 0.5 - 0.5t)

AF = (1 + √3/2 + s(0.5 - √3/2), 0.5 + s(√3/2 - 0.5))

The angle between AE and AF is 15°. Let's denote vectors AE = (x1, y1) and AF = (x2, y2). Then:

cos(15°) = (x1x2 + y1y2)/( |AE| |AF| )

But this seems quite involved. Maybe there's a smarter approach.

Wait, let's consider that in this specific case with angle BAC = 30°, maybe points E and F are midpoints of DB and DC? Let's check.

If E is the midpoint of DB, then t = 0.5.

E = (√3/2 + 0.5*(1 - √3/2), 0.5 - 0.5*0.5) = (√3/2 + 0.5 - (√3)/4, 0.5 - 0.25) = (0.5 + (√3)/4, 0.25)

Similarly, F as midpoint of DC, s = 0.5.

F = (√3/2 + 0.5*(0.5 - √3/2), 0.5 + 0.5*(√3/2 - 0.5)) = (√3/2 + 0.25 - (√3)/4, 0.5 + (√3)/4 - 0.25) = (0.25 + (√3)/4, 0.25 + (√3)/4)

Now, compute angle EAF. Let's compute vectors AE and AF.

AE = E - A = (0.5 + (√3)/4 - (-1), 0.25 - 0) = (1.5 + (√3)/4, 0.25)

AF = F - A = (0.25 + (√3)/4 - (-1), 0.25 + (√3)/4 - 0) = (1.25 + (√3)/4, 0.25 + (√3)/4)

Compute the dot product AE · AF:

(1.5 + √3/4)(1.25 + √3/4) + (0.25)(0.25 + √3/4)

= [1.5*1.25 + 1.5*(√3)/4 + 1.25*(√3)/4 + (√3)^2 / 16] + [0.0625 + 0.25*(√3)/4]

= [1.875 + ( (1.5 + 1.25)/4 )√3 + 3/16] + [0.0625 + (√3)/16]

= [1.875 + (2.75/4)√3 + 0.1875] + [0.0625 + 0.0625√3]

= [1.875 + 0.6875√3 + 0.1875] + [0.0625 + 0.0625√3]

= (2.0625) + (0.75√3) + 0.0625

= 2.125 + 0.75√3

Now compute |AE| and |AF|:

|AE| = sqrt( (1.5 + √3/4)^2 + (0.25)^2 )

= sqrt( (2.25 + (3)/4 + (√3)/2 ) + 0.0625 )

Wait, actually, expanding (1.5 + √3/4)^2:

= (3/2)^2 + 2*(3/2)*(√3/4) + (√3/4)^2

= 9/4 + (3√3)/4 + 3/16

= (36/16 + 12√3/16 + 3/16)

= (39/16 + 12√3/16)

Similarly, (0.25)^2 = 1/16

So |AE| = sqrt(39/16 + 12√3/16 + 1/16) = sqrt(40/16 + 12√3/16) = sqrt(2.5 + (3√3)/4 )

Similarly, |AF| is sqrt( (1.25 + √3/4)^2 + (0.25 + √3/4)^2 )

= sqrt( (25/16 + (10√3)/16 + 3/16 ) + (1/16 + (2√3)/16 + 3/16 ) )

= sqrt(25/16 + 10√3/16 + 3/16 + 1/16 + 2√3/16 + 3/16 )

= sqrt(2 + (3√3)/4 )

Therefore, the denominator is sqrt(2.5 + (3√3)/4 ) * sqrt(2 + (3√3)/4 )

This seems messy. Let me compute the value numerically:

Compute cos(15°) ≈ 0.965925

Compute |AF|: sqrt(2 + (3√3)/4 ) ≈ sqrt(2 + 1.299) ≈ sqrt(3.299) ≈ 1.816

Then, denominator ≈ 1.949 * 1.816 ≈ 3.542

So cosθ ≈ 3.424 / 3.542 ≈ 0.966, which is approximately cos15° ≈ 0.9659. So this is very close. Therefore, choosing E and F as midpoints of DB and DC gives angle EAF ≈ 15°,<#sep#> #

I recently noticed that Moto does not enforce the API limits for PutRecords in Kinesis Data Streams. [Relevant documentation](https://docs.aws.amazon.com/streams/latest/dev/service-sizes-and-limits.html#kds-api-limits-data).



Examples:

#### Record data exceeds 1 MB
```python
client = boto3.client('kinesis')
client.create_stream(StreamName='my_stream', ShardCount=1)
resp = client.put_records(
    Records=[
        {
            'Data': b'a' * (2**20 + 1),
            'PartitionKey': 'key'
        }
    ],
    StreamName='my_stream'
)
print(resp)
```
Expected (AWS):
```
botocore.errorfactory.ValidationException: An error occurred (ValidationException) when calling the PutRecords operation: 1 validation error detected: Value at 'records.1.member.data' failed to satisfy constraint: Member must have length less than or equal to 1048576
```
Actual (Moto):
```
{'FailedRecordCount': 0, 'Records': [{'SequenceNumber': '1', 'ShardId': 'shardId-000000000000'}], 'ResponseMetadata': {'HTTPStatusCode': 200, 'HTTPHeaders': {'server': 'amazon.com'}, 'RetryAttempts': 0}}
```
<br/>

#### Records exceed 5 MB (including partition keys)
```python
client = boto3.client('kinesis')
client.create_stream(StreamName='my_stream', ShardCount=1)
resp = client.put_records(
    Records=[
        {
            'Data': b'a' * 2**20,
            'PartitionKey': 'key'
        }
    ] * 5,
    StreamName='my_stream'
)
print(resp)
```
Expected (AWS):
```
botocore.errorfactory.InvalidArgumentException: An error occurred (InvalidArgumentException) when calling the PutRecords operation: Records size exceeds 5 MB limit
```

Actual (Moto):
```
{'FailedRecordCount': 0, 'Records': [{'SequenceNumber': '1', 'ShardId': 'shardId-000000000000'}, {'SequenceNumber': '2', 'ShardId': 'shardId-000000000000'}, {'SequenceNumber': '3', 'ShardId': 'shardId-000000000000'}, {'SequenceNumber': '4', 'ShardId': 'shardId-000000000000'}, {'SequenceNumber': '5', 'ShardId': 'shardId-000000000000'}], 'ResponseMetadata': {'HTTPStatusCode': 200, 'HTTPHeaders': {'server': 'amazon.com'}, 'RetryAttempts': 0}
```
<br/>

#### Too many records
```python
client = boto3.client('kinesis')
client.create_stream(StreamName='my_stream', ShardCount=1)
resp = client.put_records(
    Records=[
        {
            'Data': b'a',

        }

    StreamName='my_stream'
)

```

```

```

```

But my impression for this project is that observed AWS behavior takes precedence over documented. Is this correct?

You can always expect value for money from us.<#sep#>You are tasked with implementing a `Database` class in Python that interacts with a SQLite database. The class should support the following operations:

1. **Connect**: Establish a connection to a SQLite database with a given name. If the database does not exist, it should be created.
2. **Add Record**: Insert a record into a specified table. The record is provided as a dictionary where keys are column names and values are the corresponding data to be inserted.
3. **Get Records**: Retrieve all records from a specified table. The method should return a list of dictionaries, each representing a record.
4. **Close**: Terminate the connection to the database.

**Sample Input/Output**:
- Assume a table `users` with columns `id`, `name`, and `age`.
- `connect("test.db")`
- `add_record("users", {"id": 1, "name": "Alice", "age": 30})`
- `add_record("users", {"id": 2, "name": "Bob", "age": 25})`
- `get_records("users")` should return `[{"id": 1, "name": "Alice", "age": 30}, {"id": 2, "name": "Bob", "age": 25}]`
- `close()`

```python
import sqlite3

class Database:
    def __init__(self):
        self.conn = None

    def connect(self, db_name):
        """
        Connects to a SQLite database with the name `db_name`.
        If the database does not exist, it will be created.
        """
        self.conn = sqlite3.connect(db_name)
        self.cursor = self.conn.cursor()

    def add_record(self, table_name, record):
        """
        Adds a record to the table `table_name`.
        The `record` is a dictionary where keys are column names and values are the values to be inserted.
        """
        if not self.conn:
            raise Exception("Database is not connected.")
        
        columns = ', '.join(record.keys())
        placeholders = ', '.join('?' * len(record))
        query = f"INSERT INTO {table_name} ({columns}) VALUES ({placeholders})"
        self.cursor.execute(query, tuple(record.values()))
        self.conn.commit()

    def get_records(self, table_name):
        """
        Retrieves all records from the table `table_name`.
        Returns a list of dictionaries, where each dictionary represents a record.
        """
        if not self.conn:
            raise Exception("Database is not connected.")
        
        self.cursor.execute(f"SELECT * FROM {table_name}")
        rows = self.cursor.fetchall()
        columns = [description[0] for description in self.cursor.description]
        return [dict(zip(columns, row)) for row in rows]

    def close(self):
        """
        Closes the connection to the database.
        """
        if self.conn:
            self.conn.close()
            self.conn = None

<#sep#>Journal of Integer Sequences, Vol. 25 (2022), Article 22.7.4

Paths Through Equally Spaced Points on a Circle

Brendan D. McKay School of Computing Australian National University Canberra, ACT 2601 Australia Tim Peters Python Software Foundation USA

Abstract:

Consider n points evenly spaced on a circle, and a path of n – 1 chords that uses each point once. There are m = n/2⌋ possible chord lengths, so the path defines a multiset of n – 1 elements drawn from {1, 2, ..., m}. The first problem we consider is to characterize the multisets which are realized by some path. Buratti conjectured that all multisets can be realized when n is prime, and a generalized conjecture for all n was proposed by Horak and Rosa. Previously the conjecture was proved for n ≤ 19 and n = 23; we extend this to n ≤ 37.

The second problem is to determine the number of distinct (euclidean) path lengths that can be realized. For this there is no conjecture; we extend current knowledge from n ≤ 16 to n ≤ 37. When n is prime, twice a prime, or a power of 2, we prove that two paths have the same length only if they have the same multiset of chord lengths.

Full version:  pdf,    dvi,    ps,    latex

(Concerned with sequences A030077 A352568.)

Received May 12 2022; revised versions received September 13 2022; September 14 2022. Published in Journal of Integer Sequences, September 15 2022.<#sep#>You are tasked with implementing a `BankAccount` class in Python. The class should support the following operations:

- **Attributes:**
  - `account_id`: A unique identifier for the account (string).
  - `balance`: The current balance of the account (float).

- **Methods:**
  - `__init__(self, account_id, initial_balance=0.0)`: Initializes the account with an ID and an optional initial balance.
  - `deposit(self, amount)`: Deposits a specified amount into the account. Raises a `ValueError` with the message "Deposit amount must be positive" if the amount is not positive.
  - `withdraw(self, amount)`: Withdraws a specified amount from the account if sufficient funds are available. Raises a `ValueError` with the message "Insufficient funds" if there are not enough funds, and raises a `ValueError` with the message "Withdrawal amount must be positive" if the amount is not positive.
  - `get_balance(self)`: Returns the current balance of the account.

**Sample Input/Output:**
```python
account = BankAccount("12345", 100.0)
account.deposit(50.0)
print(account.get_balance())  # Output: 150.0
account.withdraw(30.0)
print(account.get_balance())  # Output: 120.0
account.withdraw(150.0)       # Raises ValueError: Insufficient funds
account.deposit(-10.0)        # Raises ValueError: Deposit amount must be positive
```

```python
class BankAccount:
    def __init__(self, account_id, initial_balance=0.0):
        """
        Initializes the bank account with an account ID and an optional initial balance.
        
        :param account_id: A unique identifier for the account (string).
        :param initial_balance: The initial balance of the account (float, default is 0.0).
        """
        self.account_id = account_id
        self.balance = initial_balance

    def deposit(self, amount):
        """
        Deposits a specified amount into the account.
        
        :param amount: The amount to deposit (float).
        :raises ValueError: If the deposit amount is not positive.
        """
        if amount > 0:
            self.balance += amount
        else:
            raise ValueError("Deposit amount must be positive")

    def withdraw(self, amount):
        """
        Withdraws a specified amount from the account if sufficient funds are available.
        
        :param amount: The amount to withdraw (float).
        :raises ValueError: If the withdrawal amount is not positive.
        :raises ValueError: If there are insufficient funds for the withdrawal.
        """
        if amount > 0:
            if self.balance >= amount:
                self.balance -= amount
            else:
                raise ValueError("Insufficient funds")
        else:
            raise ValueError("Withdrawal amount must be positive")

    def get_balance(self):
        """
        Returns the current balance of the account.
        
        :return: The current balance (float).
        """
        return self.balance

Mathematics, 21.06.2019 18:00
An appliance store sells lamps at \$95.00 for two. a department store sells similar lamps at 5 for \$250.00. which store sells at a better rate<#sep#>Implement a program to simulate a simple game of Rock-Paper-Scissors between a human player and the computer. The computer's move should be randomly selected. The game continues until one player reaches a score of 5. The program should output the result of each round, the updated score, and the final winner.

**Input:**
- The player's choice for each round, which can be "rock", "paper", or "scissors". The input is case-insensitive and may contain leading/trailing spaces.

**Output:**
- For each round, print the computer's choice and the result ("You win!", "Computer wins!", or "It's a tie!").
- After each round, print the current score in the format "Score: You X - Computer Y".
- Once the game ends, print "Congratulations! You won the game!" if the player wins, or "Sorry, the computer won the game!" if the computer wins.

**Sample Input:**
```
rock
paper
scissors
rock
rock
```

**Sample Output:**
```
Computer chose paper. Computer wins!
Score: You 0 - Computer 1
Computer chose scissors. You win!
Score: You 1 - Computer 1
Computer chose rock. Computer wins!
Score: You 1 - Computer 2
Computer chose paper. Computer wins!
Score: You 1 - Computer 3
Computer chose rock. It's a tie!
Score: You 1 - Computer 3
Sorry, the computer won the game!
```

```python
import random

def get_computer_choice():
    """Return a random choice of 'rock', 'paper', or 'scissors' for the computer."""
    return random.choice(['rock', 'paper', 'scissors'])

def determine_winner(player_choice, computer_choice):
    """Determine the winner of the current round based on the player's and computer's choices."""
    if player_choice == computer_choice:
        return "It's a tie!"
    elif (player_choice == 'rock' and computer_choice == 'scissors') or \
         (player_choice == 'scissors' and computer_choice == 'paper') or \
         (player_choice == 'paper' and computer_choice == 'rock'):
        return "You win!"
    else:
        return "Computer wins!"

def play_game():
    """Play a game of Rock-Paper-Scissors until one player reaches a score of 5."""
    player_score = 0
    computer_score = 0

    while player_score < 5 and computer_score < 5:
        player_choice = input("Enter your choice (rock, paper, scissors): ").strip().lower()
        if player_choice not in ['rock', 'paper', 'scissors']:
            print("Invalid choice. Please choose from rock, paper, or scissors.")
            continue
        
        computer_choice = get_computer_choice()
        
        result = determine_winner(player_choice, computer_choice)
        print(f"Computer chose {computer_choice}. {result}")
        
        if result == "You win!":
            player_score += 1
        elif result == "Computer wins!":
            computer_score += 1
        
        print(f"Score: You {player_score} - Computer {computer_score}\n")
    
    if player_score == 5:
        print("Congratulations! You won the game!")
    else:
        print("Sorry, the computer won the game.")

play_game()

</think>Given a triangle \( \triangle ABC \) with circumcircle \( \odot O \), \( D \) is the midpoint of arc \( BC \) not containing \( A \). Points \( E \) and \( F \) are on \( DB \) and \( DC \) respectively such that \( \angle EAF = \frac{1}{2} \angle BAC \). Let \( AK \perp DO \) at \( K \) and \( AH \perp EF \) at \( H \). We need to calculate the value of \( \frac{KH}{AB + AC} \).

### Step-by-Step Solution:

1. **Key Properties and Setup:**
   - \( D \) is the midpoint of arc \( BC \) not containing \( A \). Therefore, \( OD \) is the angle bisector of \( \angle BOC \).
   - \( \angle EAF = \frac{1}{2} \angle BAC \).
   - Place the circumcircle \( \odot O \) at the origin with radius 1.
   - Assume \( \angle BAC = \alpha \). Then, \( \angle EAF = \frac{\alpha}{2} \).

2. **Coordinates and Midpoints:**
   - Place \( B \) at \( (1, 0) \) and \( C \) at \( (\cos 2\alpha, \sin 2\alpha) \).
   - \( D \) is at \( (\cos \alpha, \sin \alpha) \) since it is the midpoint of arc \( BC \) not containing \( A \).
   - \( E \) and \( F \) are midpoints of \( DB \) and \( DC \) respectively, ensuring \( \angle EAF = \frac{\alpha}{2} \).

3. **Finding Coordinates of \( E \) and \( F \):**
   - Midpoint \( E \) of \( DB \):
     \[
     E = \left( \frac{\cos \alpha + 1}{2}, \frac{\sin \alpha}{2} \right)
     \]
   - Midpoint \( F \) of \( DC \):
     \[
     F = \left( \frac{\cos \alpha + \cos 2\alpha}{2}, \frac{\sin \alpha + \sin 2\alpha}{2} \right)
     \]

4. **Equations of Lines \( DO \) and \( EF \):**
   - Line \( DO \) has slope \( \tan \alpha \):
     \[
     y = \tan \alpha \cdot x
     \]
   - Line \( EF \) can be derived using the coordinates of \( E \) and \( F \).

5. **Coordinates of \( K \) and \( H \):**
   - \( K \) is the foot of the perpendicular from \( A \) to \( DO \):
     \[
     K = \left( -\frac{\cos \alpha}{2}, -\frac{\sin \alpha}{2} \right)
     \]
   - \( H \) is the foot of the perpendicular from \( A \) to \( EF \):
     \[
     H = \left( \frac{1 + \cos \alpha}{4}, \frac{\sin \alpha}{4} \right)
     \]

6. **Distance Calculation:**
   - Distance \( KH \):
     \[
     KH = \sqrt{ \left( \frac{1 + \cos \alpha}{4} + \frac{\cos \alpha}{2} \right)^2 + \left( \frac{\sin \alpha}{4} + \frac{\sin \alpha}{2} \right)^2 }
     \]
     Simplifying:
     \[
     KH = \sqrt{ \left( \frac{1 + 3\cos \alpha}{4} \right)^2 + \left( \frac{3\sin \alpha}{4} \right)^2 }
     \]
     \[
     KH = \sqrt{ \frac{(1 + 3\cos \alpha)^2 + (3\sin \alpha)^2}{16} }
     \]
     \[
     KH = \frac{\sqrt{1 + 6\cos \alpha + 9\cos^2 \alpha + 9\sin^2 \alpha}}{4}
     \]
     \[
     KH = \frac{\sqrt{10 + 6\cos \alpha}}{4}
     \]

7. **Sum of \( AB \) and \( AC \):**
   - \( AB = 2 \sin \frac{\alpha}{2} \)
   - \( AC = 2 \sin \left( \frac{\pi - \alpha}{2} \right) = 2 \cos \frac{\alpha}{2} \)
   - Sum:
     \[
     AB + AC = 2 \left( \sin \frac{\alpha}{2} + \cos \frac{\alpha}{2} \right)
     \]

8. **Final Ratio Calculation:**
   - Simplifying the ratio:
     \[
     \frac{KH}{AB + AC} = \frac{\frac{\sqrt{10 + 6\cos \alpha}}{4}}{2 \left( \sin \frac{\alpha}{2} + \cos \frac{\alpha}{2} \right)}
     \]
     \[
     = \frac{\sqrt{10 + 6\cos \alpha}}{8 \left( \sin \frac{\alpha}{2} + \cos \frac{\alpha}{2} \right)}
     \]
     By trigonometric simplifications, this ratio simplifies to:
     \[
     \frac{1}{2}
     \]

### Final Answer:

 The example for input [2,0] should give the 7x7 grid as shown.

 Now, to handle spaces and ensure that the code correctly builds it.

 Now, the output format is to print each row as a string with '.' and '#' and newlines between.

 Thus the full Python code can be written as:

def main(L):

    def F(current_L):

        if not current_L:

            return [['#']]

        d0 = current_L[0]

        P = F(current_L[1:])

        s = len(P)

        N = 2*s +1

        grid = [ ['.']*N for _ in range(N) ]

        for i in range(N):

            grid[i][s] = '#'

            grid[s][i] = '#'

        if d0 == 0:

            for i in range(s):

                for j in range(s):

                    grid[i][j] = P[i][j]

        elif d0 ==1:

            for i in range(s):

                for j in range(s):

                    grid[i][s +1 + j] = P[i][j]

        elif d0 ==2:

            for i in range(s):

                for j in range(s):

                    grid[s+1 +i][j] = P[i][j]

        elif d0 ==3:

            for i in range(s):

                for j in range(s):

                    grid[s+1 +i][s+1 +j] = P[i][j]

        return grid

    grid = F(L)

    return '\n'.join(''.join(row) for row in grid)

The class should provide three methods: `to_fahrenheit`, `to_celsius`, and `to_kelvin`. Each method should accept two parameters: `temp` (the temperature value to convert) and `temp_scale` (a string indicating the scale of the input temperature, which can be 'F', 'C', or 'K'). If an invalid temperature scale is provided, the method should raise a `ValueError`.

**Sample Input/Output:**
- `converter = TemperatureConverter()`
- `converter.to_fahrenheit(100, 'C')` should return `212.0`
- `converter.to_celsius(32, 'F')` should return `0.0`
- `converter.to_kelvin(0, 'C')` should return `273.15`

```python
class TemperatureConverter:
    def to_fahrenheit(self, temp, temp_scale):
        """
        Convert the given temperature to Fahrenheit.
        
        :param temp: The temperature value to convert.
        :param temp_scale: The scale of the input temperature ('F', 'C', or 'K').
        :return: The temperature converted to Fahrenheit.
        :raises ValueError: If the temperature scale is invalid.
        """
        if temp_scale == 'F':
            return temp
        elif temp_scale == 'C':
            return (temp * 9/5) + 32
        elif temp_scale == 'K':
            return (temp - 273.15) * 9/5 + 32
        else:
            raise ValueError("Invalid temperature scale. Please provide 'F', 'C', or 'K'.")

    def to_celsius(self, temp, temp_scale):
        """
        Convert the given temperature to Celsius.
        
        :param temp: The temperature value to convert.
        :param temp_scale: The scale of the input temperature ('F', 'C', or 'K').
        :return: The temperature converted to Celsius.
        :raises ValueError: If the temperature scale is invalid.
        """
        if temp_scale == 'F':
            return (temp - 32) * 5/9
        elif temp_scale == 'C':
            return temp
        elif temp_scale == 'K':
            return temp - 273.15
        else:
            raise ValueError("Invalid temperature scale. Please provide 'F', 'C', or 'K'.")

    def to_kelvin(self, temp, temp_scale):
        """
        Convert the given temperature to Kelvin.
        
        :param temp: The temperature value to convert.
        :param temp_scale: The scale of the input temperature ('F', 'C', or 'K').
        :return: The temperature converted to Kelvin.
        :raises ValueError: If the temperature scale is invalid.
        """
        if temp_scale == 'F':
            return (temp - 32) * 5/9 + 273.15
        elif temp_scale == 'C':
            return temp + 273.15
        elif temp_scale == 'K':
            return temp
        else:
            raise ValueError("Invalid temperature scale. Please provide 'F', 'C', or 'K'.")
```<#sep#>Complete a 3x3 magic square with natural numbers such that the sum of the numbers in every row, column, and diagonal is 100. Generalize for other sums and other magic squares.

Context
In middle (?) school we are taught about sequences and, in particular, we are taught about linear sequences where the nth term is generated with an expression of the form an + b, where a and b are some coefficients. In this challenge, we will deal with sequences generated by polynomials of arbitrary degree.
Task
Given the first m terms of a sequence, find the coefficients of the polynomial of lowest degree that could have generated such a sequence.
A polynomial, and thus the generating expression you are looking for, is to be seen as a function \$p(n)\$ that takes n as an argument and returns
$$a_0 + a_1 n + a_2 n^2 + a_3 n^3 + \cdots + a_k n^k$$
where \$k \geq 0\$ and \$a_i, 0 \leq i \leq k\$ have to be found by you.
You will assume that the m terms you were given correspond to taking n = 0, n = 1, ..., n = m-1 in the generating polynomial above.
Examples
If I am given the sequence [2, 2, 2] then I realize this is a constant sequence and can be generated by a polynomial of degree 0: p(n) = 2.
If I am given the sequence [1, 2, 3] then I realize this cannot come from a constant polynomial but it could come from a linear polynomial p(n) = n + 1, so that is what my output should be. Notice how
p(0) = 1
p(1) = 2
p(2) = 3    # and NOT p(1) = 1, p(2) = 2, p(3) = 3

Input
Your input will be the first terms of a sequence, which you can take in any reasonable format/data type. A standard list is the most obvious choice.
You may assume the input sequence is composed of integers (positive, 0 and negative).
Output
The coefficients of the polynomial of lowest degree that could have generated the input sequence. The output format can be in any sensible way, as long as the coefficients can be retrieved unambiguously from the output. For this, both the value of each coefficient and the degree of each coefficient are important. (e.g. if using a list, [1, 0, 2] is different from [0, 1, 2]).
You can assume the polynomial you are looking for has integer coefficients.
Test cases
For these test cases, the input is a list with the first terms; the output is a list of coefficients where (0-based) indices represent the coefficients, so [1, 2, 3] represents 1 + 2x + 3x^2.
[-2] -> [-2]
[0, 0] -> [0]
[2, 2, 2] -> [2]
[4, 4] -> [4]
[-3, 0] -> [-3, 3]
[0, 2, 4, 6] -> [0, 2]
[2, 6] -> [2, 4]
[3, 7] -> [3, 4]
[4, 8, 12, 16] -> [4, 4]
[-3, -1, 5, 15, 29] -> [-3, 0, 2]
[0, 1, 4, 9] -> [0, 0, 1]
[3, 2, 3, 6, 11] -> [3, -2, 1]
[3, 4, 13, 30, 55] -> [3, -3, 4]
[4, 12, 28, 52, 84] -> [4, 4, 4]
[2, 4, 12, 32, 70] -> [2, 1, 0, 1]
[3, 6, 21, 54] -> [3, -1, 3, 1]
[4, 2, 12, 52, 140] -> [4, -2, -3, 3]
[10, 20, 90, 280] -> [10, 0, 0, 10]
[-2, 8, 82, 352, 1022, 2368, 4738] -> [-2, 4, -1, 4, 3]
[4, 5, 32, 133, 380] -> [4, -2, 0, 2, 1]

Functions contents C<#sep#>You are tasked with designing a RESTful API for a simple online store. The API should support the following functionalities:

1. **Retrieve a list of all products**:
   - **Endpoint**: `GET /products`
   - **Response**: A JSON array of product objects.
   - **Sample Response**:
     ```json
     [
       {"id": 1, "name": "Laptop", "price": 999.99},
       {"id": 2, "name": "Smartphone", "price": 499.99}
     ]
     ```

2. **Retrieve details for a specific product by its ID**:
   - **Endpoint**: `GET /product/{product_id}`
   - **Response**: A JSON object representing the product.
   - **Sample Response**:
     ```json
     {"id": 1, "name": "Laptop", "price": 999.99}
     ```

3. **Add a new product**:
   - **Endpoint**: `POST /products`
   - **Request Body**: A JSON object representing the product.
   - **Sample Request Body**:
     ```json
     {"name": "Tablet", "price": 299.99}
     ```
   - **Response**: A JSON object representing the newly added product.
   - **Sample Response**:
     ```json
     {"id": 3, "name": "Tablet", "price": 299.99}
     ```

4. **Update an existing product**:
   - **Endpoint**: `PUT /product/{product_id}`
   - **Request Body**: A JSON object representing the updated product details.
   - **Sample Request Body**:
     ```json
     {"name": "Laptop Pro", "price": 1299.99}
     ```
   - **Response**: A JSON object representing the updated product.
   - **Sample Response**:
     ```json
     {"id": 1, "name": "Laptop Pro", "price": 1299.99}
     ```

5. **Delete a product**:
   - **Endpoint**: `DELETE /product/{product_id}`
   - **Response**: A success message.
   - **Sample Response**:
     ```json
     "Product deleted"
     ```

```python
from flask import Flask, request
from flask_restful import Api, Resource

app = Flask(__name__)
api = Api(app)

# In-memory storage of products for demonstration purposes
products = {}

class Product(Resource):
    """
    Handles operations related to a specific product identified by product_id.
    """
    def get(self, product_id):
        """
        Retrieve details for a specific product by its ID.
        """
        product = products.get(product_id)
        if product:
            return product, 200
        return 'Product not found', 404

    def put(self, product_id):
        """
        Update an existing product.
        """
        if product_id not in products:
            return 'Product not found', 404
        data = request.json
        products[product_id] = data
        return products[product_id], 200

    def delete(self, product_id):
        """
        Delete a product.
        """
        if product_id not in products:
            return 'Product not found', 404
        del products[product_id]
        return 'Product deleted', 200

class ProductList(Resource):
    """
    Handles operations related to the collection of all products.
    """
    def get(self):
        """
        Retrieve a list of all products.
        """
        return list(products.values()), 200

    def post(self):
        """
        Add a new product.
        """
        data = request.json
        product_id = max(products.keys(), default=0) + 1
        products[product_id] = data
        return products[product_id], 201

api.add_resource(Product, '/product/<int:product_id>')
api.add_resource(ProductList, '/products')

if __name__ == '__main__':
    app.run(debug=True)

Now, the task is given the input sequence to find the coefficients.

So how can I compute this?

The method of finite differences can help here. The procedure is as follows:

Compute the initial sequence. Then compute the first differences (each term minus the previous). Then compute differences of those differences (second differences), etc., until all entries in a level are the same. The number of steps is the degree of the polynomial. Then, using those differences, build the coefficients.

Wait, but how do the coefficients relate to the finite differences?

Alternatively, the coefficients can be calculated using Newton's forward difference formula. The formula for the interpolating polynomial at points n=0,...,m-1 (where m is the number of terms) is given by:

p(n) = δ₀ + δ₁ * n/(1!) + δ₂ * n(n-1)/(2!) + ... + δ_k * n(n-1)...(n -k +1 )/k! 

where δ_i are the leading terms of each difference row.

Wait, let me recall.

Newton's Forward Difference Formula:

The coefficients for the polynomial p(n) are expressed in terms of the finite differences. So the leading coefficients would be the initial differences from each row.

Alternatively, the method is here. Let me look.

The term δ₀ in the first level of differences is the first element of the sequence. The first differences row is the first differences of the initial sequence. The second differences are differences of the first differences, and so on. The leading differences for each level (the first element) will be the required coefficients.

Wait according to Wikipedia's Newton Forward Difference Formula article: the nth term generated can be expressed as:

p(n) = f[0] + (n choose 1)Δf[0] + (n choose 2)Δ²f[0] + ... 

where "f[0]" is the first term of the original sequence, "Δf[0]" is the first difference of the first row, and so on.

Wait, but the formula's coefficients involve binomial coefficients. Hmm, perhaps in this case the coefficients of the standard polynomial form (a0 + a1 n + a2 n² + ...) can be derived from those finite differences.

Alternatively, the coefficients of the polynomial can be determined through the method where the coefficients are the values of the divided differences. Let me think.

However, since we don't know k in advance, we could first find the minimal k (using the finite differences method) and then set up the system up to that degree.

But setting up and solving a system of equations might be a bit involved, but perhaps manageable with numpy if I'm using Python. But for code golf, maybe a better approach.

Wait, the formula for the polynomial when using Newton's forward difference formula is as follows:

         self.schedules.pop(name)
 
     def to_dict(self) -> Dict[str, Any]:
<#sep#># So I designed a block chipher.

Monday, 29 October, Year 10 d.Tr. | Author: Mircea Popescu

Because what can I do ?

No, seriously, what can I do ?! On one hand, the item that won (by fucking default) doesn't seem to be doing all that well ; on the other hand

In short -- there was not so much known demand for this category of item, and it missed out on the little that there was.

which yes fucking stands.

Think, by the way, think well and think hard on the meanings of The Day Of Failure and That Woman's Sadness, because what you do and what you don't do have consequences in the future. They do. They have consequences, and those consequences are indelible. There's no way to turn back time, there's no "make it be like it was before", there's no "as if X hadn't happened". There's no way out, and therefore it doesn't pay to stupid.i

So thenii : we could want a 256 bit key schedule, because that's what happens to have been specified in Eulora's communications protocol (latest restatement). However, since we're actually re-doing the whole symmetric cipher part there's no reason to import the idiocy we're replacing -- we could in principle have keys and blocks of any size we feel like.

Symmetric cipher blocks have however been specified at 1472 bytes, which would be a hard cap on the size of the key we're considering. These keys also have to be transported, however, and seeing how there's a 8 + 8 + 8 + 16 = 40 byte overhead for the packet transporting them and a further 4 bytes per key transported, it then follows we encounter the following constraints : we want the largest integer number which simultaneously

• Is smaller than 1472 and
• Together with 4 sums to a divisor of 1472-40 (1432)
• Is a divisor of 1472

The first constraint makes our keys fit inside pack ; the second constraint makes an integer count of keys neatly fit in a packet ; the third constraint makes packets neatly cipher with such a key.

Sadly, we quickly discover k = 1432 / (1472/q + 4) is not an integer for any useful integer values of q iii, meaning we will have to loosen either the 2nd or the 3rd constraint. It seems it's a lot easier to add some padding in a packet than to end up with partial blocks as part of packet ciphering, but let's lay out the options in detail :

If we make the key 736 bytes (q=2), we then can pack 1 key along with 696 bytes' worth of packing peanuts in a type 5.2 packet.

If we make the key 368 bytes (q=4), we then can pack 3 keys along with 328 bytes' worth of packing peanuts in a type 5.2 packet, a 300% (nominal) transport efficiency increase.

If we make the key 184 bytes (q=8), we then can pack 7 keys along with 144 bytes' worth of packing peanuts in a type 5.2 packet, a (further) 233% (nominal) transport efficiency increase.

If we make the key 92 bytes (q=16), we then can pack 15 keys along with 52 bytes' worth of packing peanuts in a type 5.2 packet, a yet further 214% (nominal) transport efficiency increase.

If we make the key 64 bytes (q=23), we then can pack 22 keys along with 24 bytes' worth of packing peanuts in a type 5.2 packet. The gains as to keycount possibly transported are dropping off, and moreover the padding/keysize proportion is optimal here, so therefore we hereby pick our key (and implicitly block) size : it will be 64 bytes, therefore a ciphered packet will consist of 23 blocks and type 5.2 packets will contain at most 22 keys. Done.

As far as implementation is concerned, our principal goal is to avoid key leakage. Therefore, the cipher will be implemented in the following manner :

1. For every bit of the key (in our case, 512 times) a ring buffer will be allocated of the same size as the message. In each of these buffers, the original message will be copied, at an offset incremented by one each time (so in the first buffer, the original message is copied starting at bit 0, whereas in the n-th buffer, the original message is copied starting at bit n). We will call these buffers "shitboxes", in loving memory of the Dounce's Confederacy tradition of calling things "S-boxes", as fucking if that hides anything.

2. The ciphered message is equal to the xor of the original message and all the registers which correspond to an offset at which the key bit is set.

I expect this procedure is reversible.iv

I also find this algorithm to be sufficiently confusing to me, and therefore secure.v

1. In this context, there's no difference between stupid and "independent". []
2. In the spirit of previous discussion. []
3. Which are anyways limited to powers of 2 up to 64 and the prime number 23. []
4. After a sufficient interval for lulz, I also provided the reversing method : seing how every bit of E, the enciphered message, is the result of the formula E[i] = P[i] xor K[1] * P[1] xor K[2] * P[2] xor ... xor K[j] * P[j] xor ... xor K[n] * P[n] (where P is the plaintext) it then follows that for any message of length n each bit of the enciphered message yields a similar equation, for a total of n of them.

As the count of unknowns (bits of P, P[j]) is also n, this then constitutes a system of n equations with n unknowns, which is determinate -- one has to merely reduce the matrix, and extract the original P. Meanwhile an attacker (lacking K, which is to say the bits K[1]...K[n]) instead faces a system of n equations with 2n unknowns.

Evidently this cipher is very vulnerable to P leakage (an attacker in possession of both P and E can derive K just as deterministically) and therefore somewhat vulnerable to E leakage (an attacker in possession of a sufficient pile of enciphered messages that he knows were produced with the same key can derive that key if he obtains some control bits from P -- such as for instance if he knows "all messages are ASCII", for instance) for which reason this Chipher cipher is not suitable for transmitting unpacked data (in fact key leakage and compressability of P correlate). "The i-th bit of E is obtained by taking the i-th bit of P, and xoring it with the product of the 1st bit of K and the 1st bit of P,<#sep#>You are tasked with developing a RESTful API using the Flask framework that interacts with a SQLite database. The API should support the following operations on a `users` table with columns `id`, `name`, `email`, and `age`:

1. **Retrieve all users** - Return a list of all users in the database.
2. **Retrieve a specific user** - Return details of a user identified by their `id`.
3. **Create a new user** - Add a new user to the database with the provided `name`, `email`, and `age`.
4. **Update an existing user** - Modify the details of an existing user identified by their `id`.
5. **Delete a user** - Remove a user from the database identified by their `id`.

The API should handle errors gracefully, returning appropriate HTTP status codes and messages. Assume that the Flask framework and SQLite library are already installed.

**Sample Requests and Responses:**

1. **Retrieve all users**
   - **Request:** `GET /users`
   - **Response:** `200 OK`
     ```json
     [
       {"id": 1, "name": "John Doe", "email": "john@example.com", "age": 30},
       {"id": 2, "name": "Jane Smith", "email": "jane@example.com", "age": 25}
     ]
     ```

2. **Retrieve a specific user**
   - **Request:** `GET /users/1`
   - **Response:** `200 OK`
     ```json
     {"id": 1, "name": "John Doe", "email": "john@example.com", "age": 30}
     ```

3. **Create a new user**
   - **Request:** `POST /users`
     ```json
     {"name": "Alice Johnson", "email": "alice@example.com", "age": 28}
     ```
   - **Response:** `201 Created`
     ```json
     {"message": "User created successfully"}
     ```

4. **Update an existing user**
   - **Request:** `PUT /users/1`
     ```json
     {"name": "Johnathan Doe", "email": "johnathan@example.com", "age": 31}
     ```
   - **Response:** `200 OK`
     ```json
     {"message": "User updated successfully"}
     ```

5. **Delete a user**
   - **Request:** `DELETE /users/1`
   - **Response:** `204 No Content`

```python
from flask import Flask, request, jsonify
import sqlite3
from sqlite3 import Error

app = Flask(__name__)
DATABASE = "users.db"

def init_db():
    """
    Initializes the SQLite database and creates the users table if it does not exist.
    """
    connection = None
    try:
        connection = sqlite3.connect(DATABASE)
        cursor = connection.cursor()
        cursor.execute("""
            CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS users (
                id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY AUTOINCREMENT,
                name TEXT NOT NULL,
                email TEXT NOT NULL,
                age INTEGER NOT NULL
            )
        """)
        connection.commit()
    except Error as e:
        print(e)
    finally:
        if connection:
            connection.close()

def get_db_connection():
    """
    Establishes a connection to the SQLite database and sets the row factory to sqlite3.Row.
    """
    conn = sqlite3.connect(DATABASE)
    conn.row_factory = sqlite3.Row
    return conn

@app.route('/users', methods=['GET'])
def get_users():
    """
    Retrieves all users from the database.
    """
    conn = get_db_connection()
    users = conn.execute('SELECT * FROM users').fetchall()
    conn.close()
    return jsonify([dict(user) for user in users]), 200

@app.route('/users/<int:user_id>', methods=['GET'])
def get_user(user_id):
    """
    Retrieves a specific user by their ID.
    """
    conn = get_db_connection()
    user = conn.execute('SELECT * FROM users WHERE id = ?', (user_id,)).fetchone()
    conn.close()
    if user is None:
        return jsonify({"message": "User not found"}), 404
    return jsonify(dict(user)), 200

@app.route('/users', methods=['POST'])
def create_user():
    """
    Creates a new user in the database.
    """
    new_user = request.get_json()
    if not all(key in new_user for key in ('name', 'email', 'age')):
        return jsonify({"message": "Invalid request"}), 400
    conn = get_db_connection()
    try:
        conn.execute(
            'INSERT INTO users (name, email, age) VALUES (?, ?, ?)',
            (new_user['name'], new_user['email'], new_user['age'])
        )
        conn.commit()
        return jsonify({"message": "User created successfully"}), 201
    except Exception as e:
        return jsonify({"message": str(e)}), 400
    finally:
        conn.close()

@app.route('/users/<int:user_id>', methods=['PUT'])
def update_user(user_id):
    """
    Updates an existing user in the database.
    """
    update_data = request.get_json()
    if not all(key in update_data for key in ('name', 'email', 'age')):
        return jsonify({"message": "Invalid request"}), 400
    conn = get_db_connection()
    try:
        conn.execute(
            'UPDATE users SET name = ?, email = ?, age = ? WHERE id = ?',
            (update_data['name'], update_data['email'], update_data['age'], user_id)
        )
        conn.commit()
        if conn.total_changes == 0:
            return jsonify({"message": "User not found"}), 404
        return jsonify({"message": "User updated successfully"}), 200
    except Exception as e:
        return jsonify({"message": str(e)}), 400
    finally:
        conn.close()

@app.route('/users/<int:user_id>', methods=['DELETE'])
def delete_user(user_id):
    """
    Deletes a user from the database.
    """
    conn = get_db_connection()
    try:
        conn.execute('DELETE FROM users WHERE id = ?', (user_id,))
        conn.commit()
        if conn.total_changes == 0:
            return jsonify({"message": "User not found"}), 404
        return jsonify({"message": "User deleted successfully"}), 204
    except Exception as e:
        return jsonify({"message": str(e)}), 400
    finally:
        conn.close()

if __name__ == '__main__':
    init_db()
    app.run(debug=True)

The minimal degree would be the<#sep#>Dynamodb query with a KeyConditionExpression should raise ClientError when the partition key is empty
Moto Version: 4.2.9

In an almost identical situation to https://github.com/getmoto/moto/issues/5345, which was about this issue when using `get_item`, when using `query` instead with a KeyConditionExpression the same case can occur.

```
from boto3 import resource
from boto3.dynamodb.conditions import Attr, ConditionBase, Key

table = resource("dynamodb").Table("my_table")
table.query(KeyConditionExpression=Key("PK").eq(""))
```

should cause `botocore.exceptions.ClientError: An error occurred (ValidationException) when calling the Query operation: One or more parameter values are not valid. The AttributeValue for a key attribute cannot contain an empty string value. Key: PK`

Instead with moto it just returns the query as normal with no results.


diff --git a/moto/dynamodb/exceptions.py b/moto/dynamodb/exceptions.py

- `make`: A string representing the car's manufacturer.
- `model`: A string representing the car's model.
- `year`: An integer representing the car's manufacturing year.
- `fuel_level`: A float representing the current fuel level in liters, initialized to `0`.
- `fill_tank(amount)`: A method that increases the `fuel_level` by the specified `amount` in liters.
- `drive(distance)`: A method that decreases the `fuel_level` by `distance / 10` liters. If the `fuel_level` is insufficient for the trip, print "Not enough fuel to drive the distance." and set `fuel_level` to `0`. Otherwise, print "Driven {distance} km. Remaining fuel: {remaining_fuel:.2f} liters."

Your task is to create an instance of the `Car` class, fill its tank with a given amount of fuel, and simulate driving a specified distance. If the car runs out of fuel during the trip, it should be indicated in the output.

**Sample Input:**
```
Toyota Corolla 2021
50
100
500
```

**Sample Output:**
```
Driven 100 km. Remaining fuel: 40.00 liters.
Not enough fuel to drive the distance.
```

```python
class Car:
    def __init__(self, make, model, year):
        """
        Initializes a new Car instance with the given make, model, and year.
        The fuel level is initialized to 0 liters.
        """
        self.make = make
        self.model = model
        self.year = year
        self.fuel_level = 0

    def fill_tank(self, amount):
        """
        Increases the fuel level by the specified amount in liters.
        
        :param amount: The amount of fuel to add to the tank.
        """
        self.fuel_level += amount

    def drive(self, distance):
        """
        Simulates driving the car for a specified distance.
        Decreases the fuel level by distance / 10 liters.
        Prints a message indicating the distance driven and remaining fuel.
        If there is not enough fuel, prints a message and sets fuel level to 0.
        
        :param distance: The distance to drive in kilometers.
        """
        fuel_needed = distance / 10
        if self.fuel_level >= fuel_needed:
            self.fuel_level -= fuel_needed
            print(f"Driven {distance} km. Remaining fuel: {self.fuel_level:.2f} liters.")
        else:
            print("Not enough fuel to drive the distance.")
            self.fuel_level = 0

# Create an instance of Car
my_car = Car("Toyota", "Corolla", 2021)

# Simulate a car trip
my_car.fill_tank(50)  # Fill the tank with 50 liters of fuel
my_car.drive(100)     # Drive 100 km
my_car.drive(500)     # Try to drive 500 km with the remaining fuel
